t_{1}<t_{2}
(since otherwise agent
(i_{1},2t_{1})
cannot influence agent
(i_{2},2t_{2})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
), we know
o_{i_{3},t_{1}+1}\in\tau_{i_{3},(t_{1}+1)^{-}}\subseteq\tau_{i_{3},t_{2}^{-}}
even under no additional sharing, and then we get agent
(i_{1},t_{1})
influences agent
(i_{3},t_{2})
in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{L}}
(the Dec-POMDP induced by
\mathcal{L}
). From Lemma
B.1
, it holds that
\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{3},t_{2}^{-}})
. From Assumption
II.2
and
i_{3}\neq i_{1}
, we know
\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(c_{t_{2}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{2},t_{2}^{-}}).
If
\mathcal{L}
is sQC, by Lemma
B.1
, we have
\sigma(a_{i_{1},t_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{3},t_{2}^{-}})
, and then
\sigma(a_{i_{1},t_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(c_{t_{2}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{2},t_{2}^{-}})
from Assumption
II.2
.

2) If
a_{i_{1},t_{1}}
does not influence
s_{t_{1}+1}
, then from Assumption
III.5
,
for any
t>t_{1},a_{i_{1},t_{1}}\notin\tau_{t^{-}}
and
a_{i_{1},t_{1}}\notin\tau_{t^{+}}
, and then agent
(i_{1},2t_{1})
does not influence
\widetilde{s}_{2t_{1}+1}
and
\widetilde{o}_{2t_{1}+1}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Thus
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},2t_{1}}=a_{i_{1},t_{1}}
does not influence
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,2t_{1}+1},\forall i\in[n]
, and then it does not influence
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}+1},\forall i\in[n]
. And hence, it does not influence
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,2t_{1}+2}
and
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}+2},\forall i\in[n]
, either. By recursion, we know agent
(i_{1},2t_{1})
does not influence agent
(i_{2},2t_{2})
, which leads to a contradiction to the premise of the lemma.
This completes the proof.
∎

B-B
Proof of
Lemma

III.2

Proof.

We first have the following proposition on the hardness of solving POMDPs.

Proposition B.3
.

For any
\epsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{4})
, such that computing an
\epsilon
-additive optimal strategy in POMDPs with rewards bounded in [0,1/2] is PSPACE-hard.

Proof of
Proposition

B.3
.

In the proof of
[
22
, Theorem 4.11]
, given any
\epsilon\in[0,1)
, it constructed POMDPs from the problems of Stochastic Satisfiability (SSAT) of the Quantified Boolean Formulae. The constructed instances in
[
22
]
satisfy that the reward values lie in
\{0,2\}
, and it was then proved that finding an
\epsilon
-relative approximately optimal solution in such POMDPs is PSPACE-hard. Also, one can verify that finding an
\epsilon
-additive approximately optimal solution in such POMDPs is PSPACE-hard.

Then, for any
\epsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{4})
, let
\epsilon_{1}=4\epsilon\in[0,1)
, and leverage the construction in
[
22
, Theorem 4.11]
with
\epsilon_{1}
, but scaling the the reward values by
\frac{1}{4}
such that rewards are bounded in
[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Then, finding an
\epsilon
-additive approximately optimal solution in such POMDPs (after scaling) is PSPACE-hard.
∎

Now we proceed with the proof of Lemma
III.2
based on Proposition
B.3
. Given any POMDP
\mathcal{P}=({\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{P}},\{\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}\}_{h\in[H^{\mathcal{P}}]},\{\mathbb{T}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}\}_{h\in[H^{\mathcal{P}}]},\{\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}\}_{h\in[H^{\mathcal{P}}]},\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{P}})
with rewards bounded in
[0,1/2]
, we can construct an LTC
\mathcal{L}
with any
\alpha\in(0,1)
as follows:

•

Number of agents:
n=3
; length of episode:
H=H^{\mathcal{P}}
.

•

Underlying state space:
{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{P}}\times[2]
. For any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we can split
s=(s^{1},s^{2})
, where
s^{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{P}},s^{2}\in[2]
. Initial state distribution:
\forall s\in{\mathcal{S}},\mu_{1}(s)=\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{P}}(s^{1})/2
.

•

Control action space: For any
h\in[H],\mathcal{A}_{1,h}=\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{A}_{2,h}=[2],\mathcal{A}_{3,h}=\{\emptyset\}
.

•

Transition: For any
h\in[H],s_{h},s_{h+1}\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{h}\in\mathcal{A}_{h},\mathbb{T}_{h}(s_{h+1}{\,|\,}s_{h},a_{h})=\mathbb{T}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}(s_{h+1}^{1}{\,|\,}s_{h}^{1},a_{1,h})\mathds{1}[s_{h+1}^{2}=a_{2,h}]
.

•

Observation space: For any
h\in[H],\mathcal{O}_{1,h}=\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{P}}_{h},\mathcal{O}_{2,h}=\mathcal{O}_{3,h}={\mathcal{S}}
.

•

Emission: For any
h\in[H],o_{h}\in\mathcal{O}_{h},s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}},\mathbb{O}_{h}(o_{h}{\,|\,}s_{h})=\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}(o_{1,h}{\,|\,}s_{h}^{1})\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\prime}(o_{2,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\prime}(o_{3,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})
, where
\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\prime}
is defined as

\forall o\in{\mathcal{S}},s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}},\qquad\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\prime}(o{\,|\,}s_{h})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\alpha+\frac{1-\alpha}{|{\mathcal{S}}|}\quad~&\text{if }o=s_{h}\\
&\frac{1-\alpha}{|{\mathcal{S}}|}\quad~&\text{o.w.}\end{aligned}\right..

•

Baseline sharing: null.

•

Communication action space: For any
h\in[H],\mathcal{M}_{1,h}=\mathcal{M}_{2,h}=\{0,1\}^{2h-1},\mathcal{M}_{3,h}=\{0,1\}^{h}
. For any
i\in[2],p_{i,h^{-}}\in\mathcal{P}_{i,h^{-}},\phi_{i,h}(p_{i,h^{-}},m_{i,h})=\{o_{i,k}{\,|\,}\forall k\leq h,\text{~the~}(2k-1)\text{-th digit of $m_{i,h}$ is 1 and }o_{i,k}\in p_{i,h^{-}}\}\cup\{a_{i,k}{\,|\,}\forall k\leq h-1,\text{ the }2k\text{-th digit of $m_{i,h}$ is 1 and }a_{i,k}\in p_{i,h^{-}}\}\cup\{m_{i,h}\}.
For agent
3,~p_{3,h^{-}}\in\mathcal{P}_{3,h^{-}},~\phi_{3,h}(p_{3,h^{-}},m_{3,h})=\{o_{3,k}{\,|\,}\forall k\leq h,\text{ the }k\text{-th digit of $m_{3,h}$ is 1 and }o_{3,k}\in p_{3,h^{-}}\}\cup\{m_{3,h}\}
.

•

Reward function: For any
h\in[H]
,
\forall s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{h}\in\mathcal{A}_{h},\mathcal{R}_{h}(s_{h},a_{h})=\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}(s_{h}^{1},a_{1,h})/H
.

•

Communication cost function: For any
h\in[H]
,
\forall z_{h}^{a}\in\mathcal{Z}_{h}^{a},\mathcal{K}_{h}(z_{h}^{a})=\mathds{1}[z_{h}^{a}\neq\{m_{h}\}]
. It means that the communication cost is
1
unless there is no additional sharing.

•

We restrict the communication strategy to only use
c_{h}
as input. And for any
t\in[H-1]
, we remove
a_{3,t}
in
\tau_{h^{-}},\tau_{h^{+}}
for all
h>t
.

We first verify that such a construction satisfies Assumptions
III.1
,
III.4
,
III.5
, and
III.7
, but is not QC.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumptions
III.1
,
III.7
, because both agent 2 and agent 3 have individual
\gamma
-observability with
\gamma=\alpha
. That is, for any
b_{1},b_{2}\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}}),i=2,3
, we have

\displaystyle||\mathbb{O}_{i,h}^{\top}(b_{1}-b_{2})||_{1}=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}\in\mathcal{O}_{h}}|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbb{O}_{i,h}(o_{i,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}\in\mathcal{O}_{h}}|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))(\frac{1-\alpha}{|{\mathcal{S}}|}+\alpha\mathds{1}[o_{i,h}=s_{h}])|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}\in\mathcal{O}_{h}}\alpha|b_{1}(o_{i,h})-b_{2}(o_{i,h})|=\alpha||b_{1}-b_{2}||_{1}.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumption
III.4
, because we restrict that the communication strategy can only use
c_{h}
as input.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumption
III.5
since the control actions
a_{3,t}
(for all
t\in[H-1]
) do not influence the underlying state, and we remove
a_{3,t}
from
\tau_{h}
for any
h>t
.

•

\mathcal{L}
is not QC, since for any
h\in[H-1],
agent
(2,h)
influences the state
s_{h+1}
and then influences
o_{3,h+1}
and
\tau_{3.h+1}
. However, agent
(3,h+1)
does not know the information of agent
(2,h)
, i.e.,
\sigma(\tau_{2,h})\nsubseteq\sigma(\tau_{3,h+1})
.

In this LTC problem
\mathcal{L}
, let
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})
be any
\epsilon
-team optimal strategy, with
\epsilon\in[0,1/4)
. If any agent shares any information through additional sharing, i.e.,
\exists h\in[H],\mathbb{P}(z_{h}^{a}\neq\{m_{h}\}{\,|\,}g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})>0
, we then choose the
h
to be the minimal one, i.e.,
h=\min\{h^{\prime}\in[H]{\,|\,}\mathbb{P}(z_{h^{\prime}}^{a}\neq\{m_{h^{\prime}}\}{\,|\,}g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})>0\}
being the first time the additional sharing occurs.
This means that at this timestep
h
, there is no observation or action in
c_{h^{-}}
(almost surely), since baseline sharing is null.
Then, there exists an agent
i\in[2]
such that
\mathbb{P}(z_{i,h}^{a}\neq\{m_{i,h}\}{\,|\,}g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})>0
.

From the construction of
\mathcal{L}
, we know that agent
i
chooses
m_{i,h}
based on
c_{h^{-}}
. It means it will always share, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(z_{i,h}^{a}\neq\{m_{i,h}\}{\,|\,}g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})=1
. Therefore,
\mathbb{P}(\kappa_{h}=1{\,|\,}g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})=1
, and
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})=\mathbb{E}[\sumop\displaylimits_{t=1}^{H}r_{t}-\kappa_{t}{\,|\,}g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\sumop\displaylimits_{t=1}^{H}r_{t}{\,|\,}g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast}]-\mathbb{E}[\kappa_{h}{\,|\,}g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast}]\leq H\cdot\frac{1}{2H}-1\leq-\frac{1}{2}
. Note that the rewards in
\mathcal{P}
are bounded by
[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, and the rewards in
\mathcal{L}
are bounded by
[0,\frac{1}{2H}]
. Hence,
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})
is not an
\epsilon
-team optimal for any
\epsilon\in[0,1/4)
.

Therefore, any
\epsilon
-team optimal strategy yields no additional sharing. Then, any
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})
being an
\frac{\epsilon}{H}
-team optimal strategy of
\mathcal{L}
will directly give an
\epsilon
-optimal strategy of
\mathcal{P}
as
\{g_{1,h}^{a,\ast}\}_{h\in[H]}
, since when there is no sharing, the decision process is only controlled by agent
1
. From Proposition
B.3
, we complete the proof.
∎

B-C
Proof of
Lemma

III.3

Proof.

We prove this result by showing a reduction from the Team Decision problem
[
35
]
.

Definition B.4

(Team decision problem (TDP))
.

Given finite sets
Y_{1},Y_{2},U_{1},U_{2}
, a rational probability mass function
p:Y_{1}\times Y_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
, and an integer cost function
c:Y_{1}\times Y_{2}\times U_{1}\times U_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, find decision rules
\gamma_{i}:Y_{i}\rightarrow U_{i},i=1,2
that minimize the expected cost

J(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})=\sumop\displaylimits_{y_{1}\in Y_{1},y_{2}\in Y_{2}}c(y_{1},y_{2},\gamma_{1}(y_{1}),\gamma_{2}(y_{2}))p(y_{1},y_{2}).

(B.1)

We show the NP-hardness of solving LTC from the problem of TDP, even with
|U_{1}|=|U_{2}|=2

[
35
]
. Given any TDP
{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{D}=(\widetilde{Y}_{1},\widetilde{Y}_{2},\widetilde{U}_{1},\widetilde{U}_{2},\widetilde{c},\widetilde{p},\widetilde{J})
with
|\widetilde{U}_{1}|=|\widetilde{U}_{2}|=2
, let
\widetilde{U}_{1}=\{1,2\},\widetilde{U}_{2}=\{1,2\}
, then we can construct an
H=3
and
2
-agent LTC
\mathcal{L}
with two parameters
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\alpha_{2}\in(0,1)
(to be specified later) such that:

•

Number of agents:
n=2
.

•

Underlying state:
{\mathcal{S}}=[2]^{4}
. For each
s_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we can split
s_{1}
into 4 parts as
s_{1}=(s_{1}^{1},s_{1}^{2},s_{1}^{3},s_{1}^{4})
, where
s_{1}^{1},s_{1}^{2},s_{1}^{3},s_{1}^{4}\in[2]
. Similarly,
s_{2},s_{3}\in{\mathcal{S}}
can be split in the same way.

•

Initial state distribution:
\forall s_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}},\mu_{1}(s_{1})=\frac{1}{16}
.

•

Control action space: For the first
2
timesteps,
\forall i=1,2,\mathcal{A}_{i,1}=\mathcal{A}_{i,2}=\{\emptyset\}
; for
h=3,\mathcal{A}_{1,3}=[2],\mathcal{A}_{2,3}=\{\emptyset\}
.

•

Transition:

\forall s\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2},a_{3}\in\mathcal{A}_{3},\mathbb{T}_{1}(s{\,|\,}s,a_{1})=\mathbb{T}_{2}(s{\,|\,}s,a_{2})=\mathbb{T}_{3}(s{\,|\,}s,a_{3})=1
. Note that under the transition dynamics above,
s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}
always holds, for any
s_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

Observation space:
\mathcal{O}_{1,1}=\mathcal{O}_{2,1}=\mathcal{O}_{1,2}=\mathcal{O}_{2,2}=[2]\times{\mathcal{S}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{1,3}=\widetilde{Y_{1}}\times{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{O}_{2,3}=\widetilde{Y_{2}}\times{\mathcal{S}}
. Hence, for each
i\in[2],h\in[2],o_{i,h}\in\mathcal{O}_{i,h}
, we can split
o_{i,h}
into 2 parts as
o_{i,h}=(o_{i,h}^{1},o_{i,h}^{2})
, where
o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2],o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}
. For each
i\in[n],o_{i,3}\in\mathcal{O}_{i,3}
, we can similarly split
o_{i,3}
into 2 parts as
o_{i,3}=(o_{i,3}^{1},o_{i,3}^{2})
, where
o_{i,3}^{1}\in\widetilde{Y}_{i},o_{i,3}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

Baseline sharing: null.

•

Communication action space: For
i\in[2],h\in\{1,2\},\mathcal{M}_{i,h}=\{0,1\}^{h}
;
\phi_{i,h}
is defined as:
\forall h\in\{1,2\},\phi_{i,h}(p_{i,h^{-}},m_{i,h})=\{o_{i,k}{\,|\,}\forall k\leq h,\text{the }k\text{-th}\text{ digit of $m_{i,h}$ is }1\text{ and }o_{i,k}\in p_{i,h^{-}}\}
. For
h=3
,
\mathcal{M}_{i,3}=\{1,2\}
, and if
m_{i,3}=1
, then
\phi_{i,3}(p_{i,3^{-}},m_{i,3})=\{o_{i,1},o_{i,3},m_{i,3}\}
; if
m_{i,3}=2
, then
\phi_{i,3}(p_{i,3^{-}},m_{i,3})=\{o_{i,2},o_{i,3},m_{i,3}\}
.

•

Emission:
For any
i\in[2],h\in[2],s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}},o_{i,h}\in\mathcal{O}_{i,h},\mathbb{O}_{h}(o_{h}{\,|\,}s_{h})=\Pi_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{O}_{i,h}(o_{i,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})
and
\mathbb{O}_{i,h}(o_{i,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})
is defined as:

\mathbb{O}_{i,h}(o_{i,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})=\begin{cases}\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{16}&o_{i,h}^{1}=s_{h}^{i+2h-2},o_{i,h}^{2}\neq s_{h}\\
\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{16}+\alpha_{2}&o_{i,h}^{1}=s_{h}^{i+2h-2},o_{i,h}^{2}=s_{h}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases}.

For
i\in[2],h=3
,
s_{3}\in{\mathcal{S}},o_{3}\in\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathbb{O}_{3}(o_{3}{\,|\,}s_{3})=\widetilde{p}(o_{1,3}^{1},o_{2,3}^{1})\Pi_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{O}_{i,3}^{2}(o_{i,3}^{2}{\,|\,}s_{3})
, where

\displaystyle\mathbb{O}_{i,3}^{2}(o_{i,3}^{2}{\,|\,}s_{3})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{16}&o_{i,3}^{2}\neq s_{3}\\
\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{16}+\alpha_{2}&o_{i,3}^{2}=s_{3}\\
\end{cases}.

The reward functions are defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}(s_{1},a_{1})=\mathcal{R}_{2}(s_{2},a_{2})=0,~~\forall s_{1},s_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2};

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{3}(s_{3},a_{3})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if ($a_{1,3}=s_{3}^{2}$ or $a_{1,3}=s_{3}^{4}$) and ($a_{2,3}=s_{3}^{1}$ or $a_{2,3}=s_{3}^{3}$)}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\\
\end{cases}.

The communication cost functions are defined as:

\displaystyle\forall h\in\{1,2\},z_{h}^{a}\in\mathcal{Z}_{h}^{a},\qquad\mathcal{K}_{h}(z_{h}^{a})=1\text{ if $z_{h}^{a}\neq\{m_{1,h},m_{2,h}\}$, else }0;

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{3}(z_{3}^{a})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{c}(o_{1,3}^{1},o_{2,3}^{1},1,1)/\alpha_{1}&\text{if $\{o_{1,1},o_{2,1}\}\subseteq z_{3}^{a}$ and $\{o_{1,2},o_{2,2}\}\cap z_{3}^{a}=\emptyset$}\\
\widetilde{c}(o_{1,3}^{1},o_{2,3}^{1},2,1)/\alpha_{1}&\text{if $\{o_{1,2},o_{2,1}\}\subseteq z_{3}^{a}$ and $\{o_{1,1},o_{2,2}\}\cap z_{3}^{a}=\emptyset$}\\
\widetilde{c}(o_{1,3}^{1},o_{2,3}^{1},1,2)/\alpha_{1}&\text{if $\{o_{1,1},o_{2,2}\}\subseteq z_{3}^{a}$ and $\{o_{1,2},o_{2,1}\}\cap z_{3}^{a}=\emptyset$}\\
\widetilde{c}(o_{1,3}^{1},o_{2,3}^{1},2,2)/\alpha_{1}&\text{if $\{o_{1,2},o_{2,2}\}\subseteq z_{3}^{a}$ and $\{o_{1,1},o_{2,1}\}\cap z_{3}^{a}=\emptyset$}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases},

where we let
\alpha_{0}=\max_{y_{1},y_{2},u_{1},u_{2}}\widetilde{c}(y_{1},y_{2},u_{1},u_{2})
, and set
\alpha_{1}=2\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2}\in(0,1)
.
Note that without loss of optimality, we suppose
\alpha_{1}>0
, since if
\alpha_{1}=0
, then
\widetilde{c}(y_{1},y_{2},u_{1},u_{2})=0,\forall y_{1}\in\widetilde{Y}_{1},y_{2}\in\widetilde{Y_{2}},u_{1}\in\widetilde{U}_{1},u_{2}\in\widetilde{U}_{2}
, which is a trivial instance that cannot be the one that leads to the hardness in
[
35
]
. Hence,
0\leq\kappa_{3}=\mathcal{K}_{3}(z_{3}^{a})\leq\frac{1}{2}
for any
z_{3}^{a}\in\mathcal{Z}_{3}^{a}
always holds. Also, we remove
a_{i,t}
in
\tau_{h^{-}}
and
\tau_{h^{+}}
for any
t\in[2],i\in[2],h>t
.
Under such a construction,
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the following conditions:

•

\mathcal{L}
is QC: For all
i_{1},i_{2}\in[2],h_{1},h_{2}\in[3]
with
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
, agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
(i_{2},h_{2})
because agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
cannot influence the observations of agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
, and the baseline sharing is null. For the same agent with
i_{1}=i_{2}
, the information-inclusion assumption in
II.1
(e) ensures that
\mathcal{L}
is QC.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumptions
III.1
and
III.7
: We prove this by showing that each agent
i\in[2]
satisfies
\gamma
-observability. For any
i\in[2],h\in[2],b_{1},b_{2}\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
, let

\displaystyle||\mathbb{O}_{i,h}^{\top}(b_{1}-b_{2})||_{1}=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]}\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbb{O}_{i,h}((o_{i,h}^{1},o_{i,h}^{2}){\,|\,}s_{h})\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\geq\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]}\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbb{O}_{i,h}((o_{i,h}^{1},o_{i,h}^{2}){\,|\,}s_{h})\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbbm{1}[o_{i,h}^{1}=s_{h}^{i+2h-2}](\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{16}+\alpha_{2}\mathbbm{1}[o_{i,h}^{2}=s_{h}])\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\alpha_{2}|b_{1}(o_{i,h}^{2})-b_{2}(o_{i,h}^{2})|=\alpha_{2}||b_{1}-b_{2}||_{1}.

For any
i\in[2],h=3
, the proof is similar, where we replace
o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]
with
o_{i,h}^{1}\in\widetilde{Y}_{i}
for
h=3
.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumption
III.5
, because the joint control action histories
a_{1:3}
do not influence the underlying states, and we restrict the communication and control strategies to not use them as input.

•

\mathcal{L}
does not satisfy
III.4
, since here we allow communication strategies
g_{i,h}^{a}
to take
\tau_{i,h^{-}}
as input for any
i\in[2],h\in[3]
.

We will show below that computing a team-optimal strategy of
\mathcal{L}
can give us a team-optimal strategy of
{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{D}
.
Given
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
being a team optimal strategy of
\mathcal{L}
,
firstly, it will have no additional sharing at timesteps
h=1,2
under
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
, namely, for
h=1,2,\mathbb{P}(z_{h}^{a}=\{m_{1,h},m_{2,h}\}{\,|\,}g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})=1
. If not, then for any
i\in[2]
, consider
g_{i,1}^{m,^{\prime}},g_{i,2}^{m,^{\prime}},g_{i,3}^{a,\ast}
defined as

\displaystyle\forall\tau_{i,1^{-}},\tau_{i,2^{-}},\tau_{i,3^{+}},g_{i,1}^{m,^{\prime}}(\tau_{i,1^{-}})=(0),\quad g_{i,2}^{m,^{\prime}}(\tau_{i,2^{-}})=(0,0),\quad g_{i,3^{+}}^{a,^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}o_{3-i,1}^{1}&\text{if $o_{3-i,1}\in\tau_{i,3^{+}}$}\\
o_{3-i,2}^{1}&\text{o.w.}\end{cases},

and replace the
g_{1:2,1}^{m,\ast},g_{1:2,2}^{m,\ast},g_{1:2,3}^{a,\ast}
by
g_{1:2,1}^{m,^{\prime}},g_{1:2,2}^{m,^{\prime}},g_{1:2,3}^{a,^{\prime}}
in
g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast}
to get
(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
. It is easy to verify that
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})<J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
, since
g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}}
can guarantee
r_{3}=1
always holds; if there is no additional sharing under
g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}}
at first two timesteps, these two strategies has the same communication cost, otherwise
(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
has
\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}\kappa_{h}=\kappa_{3}\leq\frac{1}{2}
but
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
has
\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}\kappa_{h}\geq 1
. This leads to the contradiction that
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
is a team-optimal strategy.

Also, if we replace the
g_{1:2,3}^{a,\ast}
by
g_{1:2,3}^{a,^{\prime}}
, the communication cost does not change and the reward can achieve optimal, i.e.,
r_{3}=1
always holds. Thus, without loss of generality, we can assume that
g_{1:2,3}^{a,\ast}=g_{1:2,3}^{a,^{\prime}}
, since otherwise we can do the replacement and it is still a team-optimal strategy.

Therefore,
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})=\mathbb{E}[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}r_{h}-\kappa_{h}{\,|\,}g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast}]=1-\mathbb{E}[\kappa_{3}{\,|\,}g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast}]=1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{c}(o_{1,3}^{1},o_{2,3}^{1},m_{1,3},m_{2,3})]
, where
m_{1,3}=g_{1,3}^{m,\ast}(\{\tau_{1,3^{-}}\}),m_{2,3}=g_{2,3}^{m,\ast}(\{\tau_{2,3^{-}}\})
, which means
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
can minimize
\kappa_{3}
through choosing
m_{3}
properly, if there is no additional sharing at first two timesteps, i.e.,
\tau_{i,3^{-}}=\{o_{i,1},o_{i,2},o_{i,3},m_{1:2}\}
. By construction,
\kappa_{3}
only depends on
o_{3}
and
m_{3}
and is irrelevant of
\{o_{1,1},o_{1,2},o_{1,3}^{2}\}
, and
m_{i,1}=(0),m_{i,2}=(0,0),\forall i\in[2]
always hold. Hence,
\{o_{1,1},o_{1,2},o_{1,3}^{2}\}
are useless information for agent
1
to choose
m_{1,3}
and minimize
\mathbb{E}[\kappa_{3}]
. Therefore, not using them in
g_{1,3}^{m,\ast}
does not lose any optimality. Hence, we can consider the
g_{1,3}^{m,\ast}
that only has
o_{1,3}^{1}
as input. In the same way, we can consider the
g_{2,3}^{m,\ast}
that only has
o_{2,3}^{1}
as input. Therefore,
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})=1-\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{1,3}^{1},o_{1,3}^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\widetilde{c}(o_{1,3}^{1},o_{2,3}^{1},g_{1,3}^{m,\ast}(o_{1,3}^{1}),g_{2,3}^{m,\ast}(o_{2,3}^{1}))\widetilde{p}(o_{1,3}^{1},o_{2,3}^{1})
. Further, we can leverage
\widetilde{\gamma}^{\ast}_{1}=g_{1,3}^{m,\ast},\widetilde{\gamma}^{\ast}_{2}=g_{2,3}^{m,\ast}
to minimize the expected cost
\widetilde{J}
of the TDP. Therefore,
from the NP-hardness of TDPs
[
35
, Corollary 4.1]
, we complete our proof.
∎

B-D
Proof of
Lemma

III.6

Proof of Lemma
III.6
.

We prove this result by showing a reduction from the Team Decision problem. Given any TDP
{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{D}=(\widetilde{Y}_{1},\widetilde{Y}_{2},\widetilde{U}_{1},\widetilde{U}_{2},\widetilde{c},\widetilde{p},\widetilde{J})
with
|\widetilde{U}_{1}|=|\widetilde{U}_{2}|=2
, let
\widetilde{U}_{1}=\{1,2\},\widetilde{U}_{2}=\{1,2\}
, then we can construct an
H=3
and
2
-agent LTC
\mathcal{L}
with two parameters
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\alpha_{2}\in(0,1)
(to be specified later) such that:

•

Underlying state:
{\mathcal{S}}=[2]^{2}
. For each
s_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we can split
s_{1}
into 2 parts as
s_{1}=(s_{1}^{1},s_{1}^{2})
, where
s_{1}^{1},s_{1}^{2}\in[2]
. Similarly,
s_{2},s_{3}\in{\mathcal{S}}
can be split in the same way.

•

Initial state distribution:
\forall s_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}},\mu_{1}(s_{1})=\frac{1}{4}
.

•

Control action space: For any
i\in[2],h=1
,
\mathcal{A}_{i,1}=\{\emptyset\}
; for
h=2
,
\mathcal{A}_{i,2}=\{(0,x),(x,0){\,|\,}x\in[2]\}
; We can write
a_{i,2}=(a_{i,2}^{1},a_{i,2}^{2}),a_{i,2}^{1},a_{i,2}^{2}\in\{0,1,2\}
; for
h=3,\mathcal{A}_{i,3}=[2]

•

Transition:

\forall s\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2},a_{3}\in\mathcal{A}_{3},\mathbb{T}_{1}(s{\,|\,}s,a_{1})=\mathbb{T}_{2}(s{\,|\,}s,a_{2})=\mathbb{T}_{3}(s{\,|\,}s,a_{3})=1
. Note that under the transition dynamics above,
s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=s_{4}
always holds, for any
s_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

Observation space:
\mathcal{O}_{1,1}=\mathcal{O}_{2,1}=[2]\times{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{O}_{1,2}=\widetilde{Y_{1}}\times{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{O}_{2,2}=\widetilde{Y_{2}}\times{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{O}_{3,1}=\mathcal{O}_{3,2}={\mathcal{S}}
. For each
i\in[2],o_{i,1}\in\mathcal{O}_{i,1}
, we can split
o_{i,1}
into 2 parts as
o_{i,1}=(o_{i,1}^{1},o_{i,1}^{2})
, where
o_{i,1}^{1}\in[2],o_{i,1}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}
. For each
i\in[2],o_{i,2}\in\mathcal{O}_{i,2}
, similarly, we can split
o_{i,2}
into 2 parts as
o_{i,2}=(o_{i,2}^{1},o_{i,2}^{2})
, where
o_{i,2}^{1}\in\widetilde{Y}_{i},o_{i,2}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

The baseline sharing is null.

•

Communication action space: For
i\in[2],h\in\{1,2\},\mathcal{M}_{i,h}=\{0,1\}^{2h-1}
and
\phi_{i,h}
is defined as
\phi_{i,h}(p_{i,h^{-}},m_{i,h})=\{o_{i,k}\in p_{i,h^{-}}{\,|\,}\forall k\leq h,\text{the~}(2k-1)\text{-th digit of $m_{i,h}$ is }1\}\cup\{a_{i,k}\in p_{i,h^{-}}{\,|\,}\forall k\leq h-1,\text{the~}2k\text{-th digit of $m_{i,h}$ is }1\}\cup\{m_{i,h}\}
; For
h=3,\mathcal{M}_{i,3}=\{1,2\},\forall p_{i,3^{-}}\in\mathcal{P}_{i,3^{-}}
,
\phi_{i,3}(p_{i,3^{-}},1)=\{o_{i,2},a_{i,2},m_{i,3}\}
and
\phi_{i,3}(p_{i,3^{-}},2)=\{o_{i,2},a_{i,2},o_{i,3},m_{i,3}\}
.

•

Emission:
For
h=1,\forall s_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}},o_{i,1}\in\mathcal{O}_{i,1},\mathbb{O}_{1}(o_{1}{\,|\,}s_{1})=\Pi_{j=1}^{2}\mathbb{O}_{j,1}(o_{j,1}{\,|\,}s_{1})
and
\forall i\in[2],\mathbb{O}_{i,1}(o_{i,1}{\,|\,}s_{1})
is defined as:

\mathbb{O}_{i,h}(o_{i,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})=\begin{cases}\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{4}&o_{i,h}^{1}=s_{h}^{i+2h-2},o_{i,h}^{2}\neq s_{h}\\
\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{4}+\alpha_{2}&o_{i,h}^{1}=s_{h}^{i+2h-2},o_{i,h}^{2}=s_{h}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases},

for
h=2,\forall s_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}},o_{2}\in\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathbb{O}_{2}(o_{2}{\,|\,}s_{2})=\widetilde{p}(o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1})\Pi_{j=1}^{2}\mathbb{O}_{j,2}^{2}(o_{j,2}^{2}{\,|\,}s_{2})
, and
\forall i\in[2],\mathbb{O}_{i,2}^{2}(o_{i,2}{\,|\,}s_{2})
is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{O}_{i,2}^{2}(o_{2}^{2}{\,|\,}s_{2})=\begin{cases}\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{4}&o_{i,2}^{2}\neq s_{2}\\
\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{4}+\alpha_{2}&o_{i,2}^{2}=s_{2}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases},

for
h=3,\forall s_{3}\in{\mathcal{S}},o_{i,3}\in\mathcal{O}_{i,3},\mathbb{O}_{3}(o_{3}{\,|\,}s_{3})=\Pi_{j=1}^{2}\mathbb{O}_{j,3}(o_{j,3}{\,|\,}s_{3})
, and
\forall i\in[2],\mathbb{O}_{i,3}(o_{i,3}{\,|\,}s_{3})
is defined as:

\mathbb{O}_{i,3}(o_{i,3}{\,|\,}s_{3})=\begin{cases}\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{4}&o_{i,3}\neq s_{3}\\
\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{4}+\alpha_{2}&o_{i,3}=s_{3}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases}.

•

Reward functions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}(s_{1},a_{1})=\mathcal{R}_{2}(s_{2},a_{2})=0,~~\forall s_{1},s_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2};

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{3}(s_{3},a_{3})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $a_{1,3}=s_{3}^{2}$ and $a_{2,3}=s_{3}^{1}$}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\\
\end{cases}.

•

Communication cost functions:

\displaystyle\forall h\in[2],z_{h}^{a}\in\mathcal{Z}_{h}^{a},\mathcal{K}_{h}(z_{h}^{a})=1\qquad\text{ if $z_{h}^{a}\neq\{m_{h}\}$,~}\text{else }0;

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{3}(z_{3}^{a})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{c}(o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1},1,1)/\alpha_{1}&\text{if }a_{1,2},a_{2,2}\in z_{2}^{a},a_{1,2}^{1}=0,a_{2,2}^{1}=0\\
\widetilde{c}(o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1},2,1)/\alpha_{1}&\text{if }a_{1,2},a_{2,2}\in z_{3}^{a},a_{1,2}^{2}=0,a_{2,2}^{1}=0\\
\widetilde{c}(o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1},1,2)/\alpha_{1}&\text{if }a_{1,2},a_{2,2}\in z_{3}^{a},a_{1,2}^{1}=0,a_{2,2}^{2}=0\\
\widetilde{c}(o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1},2,2)/\alpha_{1}&\text{if }a_{1,2},a_{2,2}\in z_{3}^{a},a_{1,2}^{2}=0,a_{2,2}^{2}=0\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases}.

Let
\alpha_{0}=\max_{y_{1},y_{2},u_{1},u_{2}}\widetilde{c}(y_{1},y_{2},u_{1},u_{2})
, which is supposed to be positive without loss of optimality (see a discussion in §
B-C
).
We set
\alpha_{1}=2\alpha_{0}
, and hence for any
z_{3}^{a}\in\mathcal{Z}_{3}^{a}
,
\mathcal{K}_{3}(z_{3}^{a})\leq\frac{1}{2}
always holds. We set
\alpha_{2}\in(0,1)
. Also, we restrict agents to decide their communication strategies only based on their common information.
Under such a construction,
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the following conditions:

•

\mathcal{L}
is QC: For any
i_{1},i_{2}\in[2],h_{1},h_{2}\in[3]
, agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
(i_{2},h_{2})
, because agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
cannot influence the observation of agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
, and the baseline sharing is null.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumptions
III.1
and
III.7
: We prove this by showing that each agent
i\in[2]
satisfies
\gamma
-observability. For any
i\in[2],h=1,b_{1},b_{2}\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
, we have

\displaystyle||\mathbb{O}_{i,h}^{\top}(b_{1}-b_{2})||_{1}=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]}\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbb{O}_{i,h}((o_{i,h}^{1},o_{i,h}^{2}){\,|\,}s_{h})\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\geq\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]}\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbb{O}_{i,h}((o_{i,h}^{1},o_{i,h}^{2}){\,|\,}s_{h})\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbbm{1}[o_{i,h}^{1}=s_{h}^{i+2h-2}](\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{4}+\alpha_{2}\mathbbm{1}[o_{i,h}^{2}=s_{h}])\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\frac{1-\alpha_{2}}{4}(\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h})))+\alpha_{2}(b_{1}(o_{i,h}^{2})-b_{2}(o_{i,h}^{2}))\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{i,h}^{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\alpha_{2}|b_{1}(o_{i,h}^{2})-b_{2}(o_{i,h}^{2})|=\alpha_{2}||b_{1}-b_{2}||_{1}.

For any
i\in[2],h=2,3
, the proof is similar, by replacing
o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]
with
o_{i,h}^{1}\in\widetilde{Y}_{i}
for
h=2
and replacing the space
o_{i,h}^{1}\in[2]
with
\{\emptyset\}
for
h=3
.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumption
III.4
, since we restrict agents to decide their communication actions only based on the common information.

•

\mathcal{L}
does not satisfy
III.5
, since the joint control action histories
a_{1:3}
do not influence the underlying states, but they are not removed in the information.

Now, we show that any team-optimal strategy of
\mathcal{L}
will give us the decision rules
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
to solve
{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{D}
.
Given
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
being a team optimal strategy of
\mathcal{L}
, we can construct
(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
as

\displaystyle\forall i\in[2],\forall\tau_{i,1^{-}},\tau_{i,2^{-}},\tau_{i,3^{-}},g_{i,1}^{m,^{\prime}}(\tau_{i,1^{-}})=(0),\quad g_{i,2}^{m,^{\prime}}(\tau_{i,2^{-}})=(0,0,0),g_{i,3}^{m,^{\prime}}(\tau_{i,3^{-}})=1

\displaystyle\forall i\in[2],\forall\tau_{i,2^{+}},g_{i,2}^{a,^{\prime}}(\tau_{i,2^{+}})=\begin{cases}(0,o_{i,1})&\text{if the first term of $[g_{i,2^{+}}^{a,^{\prime}}(\tau_{i,2^{+}})]$ is 1}\\
(o_{i,1},0)&\text{o.w.}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\forall i\in[2],\forall\tau_{i,3^{+}},g_{i,3}^{a,^{\prime}}(\tau_{i,3^{+}})=\begin{cases}a_{3-i,2}^{2}&\text{if $a_{3-i,2}^{1}=0$}\\
a_{3-i,2}^{1}&\text{o.w.}\end{cases}.

One can verify that
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})\leq J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
. This is because,
(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
can always achieve
r_{3}=1
, which means
\mathbb{E}[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}r_{h}{\,|\,}(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})]\geq\mathbb{E}[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}r_{h}{\,|\,}g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast}]
. Also, at timesteps
h=1,2
,
(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
has no additional sharing; if
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
has additional sharing, then the communication cost
\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}\kappa_{h}
is at least 1; if
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
has no additional sharing, then
(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})
and
(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
has the same communication cost of
\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}\kappa_{h}=\kappa_{3}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}\kappa_{h}{\,|\,}(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})]\leq\mathbb{E}[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}\kappa_{h}{\,|\,}g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast}]
, and thus
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,\ast},g_{1:3}^{m,\ast})\leq J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
.

From the above, we know that
(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})
is also a team-optimal strategy. Let
U_{1}=f_{1}(a_{1,2}):=2-\mathds{1}[a_{1,2}^{1}=0],U_{2}=f_{2}(a_{2,2}):=2-\mathds{1}[a_{2,2}^{1}=0]
, then
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})=\mathbb{E}[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{3}r_{h}-\kappa_{h}{\,|\,}g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}}]=1-\mathbb{E}[\kappa_{3}{\,|\,}g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}}]=1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{c}(o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1},U_{1},U_{2})]
, where
U_{1}=f_{1}(a_{1,2})=f_{1}(g_{1,2}^{a,^{\prime}}(\tau_{1,2^{+}})),U_{2}=f_{2}(a_{2,2})=f_{2}(g_{2,2}^{a,^{\prime}}(\tau_{2,2^{+}}))
, which means that
(g_{1,2}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{2,2}^{a,^{\prime}})
can minimize
\kappa_{3}
through properly choosing
a_{2}
(and
U_{1:2}
) if there is no additional sharing in the first two timesteps, i.e.,
\tau_{i,2^{+}}=\{o_{i,1},a_{i,1},o_{i,2},m_{1:2}\}
. By construction,
\kappa_{3}
only depends on
o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1}
, and
U_{1:2}
, and is irrelevant of
\{o_{1,1},a_{1,1},o_{1,2}^{2}\}
, and
m_{i,1}=(0),m_{i,2}=(0,0,0),\forall i\in[2]
always hold. Hence,
\{o_{1,1},o_{1,2},o_{1,3}^{2}\}
are useless information for agent
1
to choose
a_{1,2}
(and
U_{1}
), and to minimize
\mathbb{E}[\kappa_{3}]
. Therefore, not using them in
g_{1,2}^{a,^{\prime}}
does not lose any optimality. Hence, we can consider the
g_{1,2}^{a,^{\prime}}
that only has
o_{1,2}^{1}
as input. In the same way, we can consider the
g_{2,2}^{a,^{\prime}}
that only has
o_{2,2}^{1}
as input. Therefore,
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:3}^{a,^{\prime}},g_{1:3}^{m,^{\prime}})=1-\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{1,3}^{1},o_{1,3}^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\widetilde{c}(o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1},f_{1}(g_{1,2}^{a,^{\prime}}(o_{1,2}^{1})),f_{2}(g_{2,2}^{a,^{\prime}}(o_{2,2}^{1})))\widetilde{p}(o_{1,2}^{1},o_{2,2}^{1})
. Further, we can leverage
\widetilde{\gamma}^{\ast}_{1}=f_{1}\circ g_{1,2}^{a,^{\prime}},\widetilde{\gamma}^{\ast}_{2}=f_{2}\circ g_{2,2}^{a,^{\prime}}
to minimize the expected cost
\widetilde{J}
of the TDP, where
\circ
denotes function composition. Therefore,
from the NP-hardness of TDPs
[
35
, Corollary 4.1]
, we complete our proof.
∎

B-E
Proof of
Lemma

III.8

Proof.

We prove this by showing a reduction from the hardness of finding an
\epsilon
-optimal strategy for POMDPs (
Proposition

B.3
). Given any POMDP
\mathcal{P}=({\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{P}},\{\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}\}_{h\in[H^{\mathcal{P}}]},\{\mathbb{T}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}\}_{h\in[H^{\mathcal{P}}]},\{\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}\}_{h\in[H^{\mathcal{P}}]},\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{P}})
with rewards bounded in
[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, we can construct an LTC
\mathcal{L}
with 2 agents as follows:

•

Number of agents:
n=2
; length of episode:
H=H^{\mathcal{P}}
.

•

{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{P}}\times[2];
for any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we can split state as two parts:
s=(s^{1},s^{2}),s^{1}\in{\mathcal{S}},s^{2}\in[2]
.

•

Initial state distribution:
\forall s_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}},\mu_{1}(s_{1})=\frac{\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{P}}(s_{1}^{1})}{2}
.

•

Control action space: For any
h\in[H]
,
\mathcal{A}_{1,h}=\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{h},\mathcal{A}_{2,h}=[2]
.

•

Transition: For any
h\in[H]
,
\forall s_{h},s_{h+1}\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{h}\in\mathcal{A}_{h},\mathbb{T}_{h}(s_{h+1}{\,|\,}s_{h},a_{h})=\mathbb{T}^{\mathcal{P}}_{h}(s_{h+1}^{1}{\,|\,}s_{h}^{1},a_{1,h})\mathds{1}[s_{h+1}^{2}=a_{2,h}]
.

•

Observation space: For any
h\in[H]
,
\mathcal{O}_{1,h}=\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{O}_{2,h}={\mathcal{S}}
.

•

Emission: For any
h\in[H],\forall o_{h}\in\mathcal{O}_{h},s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
\mathbb{O}_{h}(o_{h}{\,|\,}s_{h})=\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}(o_{1,h}{\,|\,}s_{h}^{1})\mathds{1}[o_{2,h}=s_{h}]
.

•

Reward functions: For any
h\in[H]
,
\forall s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{h}\in\mathcal{A}_{h},\mathcal{R}_{h}(s_{h},a_{h})=\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathcal{P}}(s_{h}^{1},a_{1,h})/H
.

•

Baseline sharing: For any
h\in[H],z_{h}^{b}=\{o_{1,h},a_{1,h-1}\}
.

•

Communication action space: For any
h\in[H],\mathcal{M}_{1,h}=\{\emptyset\},\mathcal{M}_{2,h}=\{0,1\}^{2h-1}
. For any
p_{1,h^{-}}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,h^{-}},p_{2,h^{-}}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,h^{-}},m_{h}\in\mathcal{M}_{h},\phi_{1,h}(p_{1,h^{-}},m_{1,h})=\{m_{1,h}\},\phi_{2,h}(p_{2,h^{-}},m_{2,h})=\{o_{2,k}{\,|\,}\forall k\leq h,\text{ the }2k-1\text{-th digit of $m_{2,h}$ is 1 and }o_{2,k}\in p_{2,h^{-}}\}\cup\{a_{2,k}{\,|\,}\forall k<h,\text{ the }2k\text{-th digit of $m_{2,h}$ is 1 and }a_{2,k}\in p_{2,h^{-}}\}\cup\{m_{2,h}\}.

•

Communication cost functions: For any
h\in[H],z_{h}^{a}\in\mathcal{Z}_{h}^{a},\mathcal{K}_{h}(z_{h}^{a})=\mathds{1}[z_{h}^{a}\neq\{m_{h}\}]
, which means that the communication cost is 1 unless there is no additional sharing.

•

We restrict the communication strategy to only use
c_{h}
as input.

We first verify that
\mathcal{L}
is QC and satisfies Assumptions
III.1
,
III.4
, and
III.5
.

•

\mathcal{L}
is QC: For any
\forall h_{1}<h_{2}\leq H
, agent
(2,h_{1})
does not influence agent
(1,h_{2})
under baseline sharing, since agent
(2,h_{1})
does not influence
s_{h}^{1},\forall h\in[H]
, then does not influence
o_{1,h},\forall h\in[H]
. Also, agent 2 shares nothing via baseline sharing. Therefore, agent
(2,h_{1})
does not influence agent
(1,h_{2})
.
For any
h_{1}<h_{2}\leq H
, under baseline sharing,
p_{1,h_{1}^{-}}=\emptyset
. Then, we have
\sigma(\tau_{1,h_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(c_{h_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(c_{h_{2}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{2,h_{2}^{-}})
.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumption
III.1
: For any
h\in[H],b_{1},b_{2}\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
,
\mathbb{O}_{h}
satisfies that

\displaystyle||\mathbb{O}_{h}^{\top}(b_{1}-b_{2})||_{1}=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{1,h}\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{P}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{2,h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbb{O}_{h}((o_{1,h},o_{2,h}){\,|\,}s_{h})\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\geq\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{2,h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{1,h}\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{P}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbb{O}_{1,h}(o_{1,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})\mathbb{O}_{2,h}(o_{2,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{2,h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathbb{O}_{2,h}(o_{2,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{1,h}\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbb{O}_{1,h}(o_{1,h}{\,|\,}s_{h})\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{2,h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg|\sumop\displaylimits_{s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}(b_{1}(s_{h})-b_{2}(s_{h}))\mathds{1}[o_{2,h}=s_{h}]\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{o_{2,h}\in{\mathcal{S}}}|b_{1}(o_{2,h})-b_{2}(o_{2,h})|=||b_{1}-b_{2}||_{1},

showing that
\gamma
-observability is satisfied with
\gamma=1
.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumption
III.4
: For any
h\in[H]
, we restricted that each agent
i
decides
m_{i,h}
based on
c_{h}
only.

•

\mathcal{L}
satisfies Assumption
III.5
: For any
h\in[H-1],a_{1,h}
influences
s_{h+1}^{1}
,
a_{2,h}
influences
s_{h+1}^{2}
.

•

\mathcal{L}
does not satisfy
III.7
. This is because for any
h\in[H]
and fixed
s_{h}^{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{P}}
, we can define
s^{1}=(s_{h}^{1},1),s^{2}=(s_{h}^{1},2)
, and
b_{1}=\delta_{s^{1}}\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}}),b_{2}=\delta_{s^{2}}\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
, where
\delta_{s^{1}},\delta_{s^{2}}
denote the distributions over
{\mathcal{S}}
only with mass on
s^{1},s^{2}
, respectively. Then, one can verify that
b_{1}\neq b_{2}
but
\mathbb{O}_{-1,h}^{\top}b_{1}=\mathbb{O}_{-1,h}^{\top}b_{2}
.

Consider the communication strategy
g_{1:H}^{m,^{\prime}}
that yields no additional sharing, namely,
\forall h\in[H],\tau_{2,h^{-}}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{2,h^{-}},g_{2,h}^{m,^{\prime}}(\tau_{2,h^{-}})=\bm{0}_{h}
. For any
\epsilon
-optimal strategy
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})
with
\epsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{4})
, we claim that
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,^{\prime}})
is also an
\epsilon
-optimal strategy.

This is because, comparing to strategy
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,^{\prime}})
, for the trajectories where
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})
leads to no additional sharing, these two strategies output the same actions and gain the same total return; for the other trajectories where
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})
leads to some additional sharing, i.e.,
z_{h}\neq\{m_{h}\}
for some
h\in[H]
, then it has the return of
\sumop\displaylimits_{t=1}^{H}(r_{t}-\kappa_{t})\leq\sumop\displaylimits_{t=1}^{H}(r_{t})-\kappa_{h}\leq\frac{1}{2H}\cdot H-1<0
, which is less than when replacing it by
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,^{\prime}})
with return
\sumop\displaylimits_{t=1}^{H}(r_{t}-\kappa_{t})=\sumop\displaylimits_{t=1}^{H}r_{t}\geq 0
.

Therefore,
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,^{\prime}})\geq J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,\ast})
, and
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,^{\prime}})
is also an
\epsilon
-optimal strategy with
\epsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{4})
.

Meanwhile, any
(g_{1:H}^{a,\ast},g_{1:H}^{m,^{\prime}})
being an
\frac{\epsilon}{H}
-team optimal strategy of
\mathcal{L}
will directly give an
\epsilon
-team-optimal strategy of
\mathcal{P}
as
\{g_{1,h}^{a,\ast}\}_{h\in[H]}
, since when there is no sharing, the decision process is only controlled by agent
1
. From Proposition
B.3
, we complete the proof.
∎

Remark B.5
.

For each construction in the proofs of
Lemmas

III.2
,
III.3
,
III.6
and
III.8
, if we slightly change the construction to make it satisfy the corresponding assumption, then the associated hardness can be avoided. For example, if we require the constructed instance in the proof of
Lemma

III.3
to satisfy
III.4
, i.e., the communication strategy
g_{1:3}^{a}
only takes common information as input. Then, finding the optimal communication strategy at timestep
h=3
can no longer be reduced to TDPs, and such LTC problems can be computed via
Algorithm

1
in polynomial time.

Appendix C
Deferred Details of §
IV

C-A
Proof of
Proposition

IV.1

Proof.

Given any strategy
(g_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m}),g_{1:H}^{a}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m}
, we can define
\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}}=(g^{m}_{1},g^{a}_{1},\cdots,g^{m}_{H},g^{a}_{H})
. From the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, comparing to applying
(g_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m})
in
\mathcal{L}
, if we apply
\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, then it is easy to verify that
\forall h\in H,\widetilde{r}_{2h-1}=-\kappa_{h},\widetilde{r}_{2h}=r_{h}
always holds. Therefore,
J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}})=J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m})
. In the same way, for any strategy
\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}}
, we can define
\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}}=(g^{m}_{1},g^{a}_{1},\cdots,g^{m}_{H},g^{a}_{H})
, and verify that
J_{\mathcal{L}}(g_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m})=J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}})
.
∎

C-B
Proof of
Theorem

IV.2

Proof.

Firstly, we prove the QC case. To show
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
is QC, we need to prove that
\forall i_{1},i_{2}\in[n],h_{1},h_{2}\in[\widetilde{H}]
, if agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
with
h_{1}<h_{2}
, then
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
, where we use
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
to denote the information available to agent
(i,h)
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
We prove it by considering the following cases:

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1}-1
with
t_{1}\in[H]
, by the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
and Assumption
III.4
, we have
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\widetilde{c}_{h_{1}}=c_{t_{1}^{-}}\subseteq\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}
, since common information accumulates over time by definition, and will always be included in the available information
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
in later steps with
h>h_{1}
. Thus,
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1},h_{2}=2t_{2}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in[H]
, then
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{+}}=\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{-}}\cup z_{t_{1}}^{a}
by definition. Consider agent
(i_{1},t_{1})
and
(i_{2},t_{2})
in
\mathcal{L}
. From , we know that if any agent in a control step influences another agent in a control step in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, then the former also influences the latter in
\mathcal{L}
under baseline sharing, and thus
\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{2},t_{2}^{-}})
since
\mathcal{L}
is QC. This further implies
\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{2},t_{2}^{+}})
since
\sigma(\tau_{i_{2},t_{2}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{2},t_{2}^{+}})
.
Also,
z_{t_{1}}^{a}\subseteq c_{t_{1}^{+}}\subseteq c_{t_{2}^{+}}\subseteq\tau_{i_{2},t_{2}^{+}}=\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}
by the accumulation of
c_{h^{+}}
over time. Thus, we have
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1},h_{2}=2t_{2}-1,t_{1},t_{2}\in[H]
, then
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}=\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}}
. Hence,
\exists i_{3}\in[n],i_{3}\neq i_{1}
, such that
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}\subseteq\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}+1}\subseteq\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{2}+1}
. Since agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
, we know that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
also influences agent
(i_{3},h_{2}+1)
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
From
Lemma

B.2
, similarly as above, we know
\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{3},t_{2}^{-}})
since
\mathcal{L}
is QC. From Assumption
II.2
and
i_{1}\neq i_{3}
, we know
\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(c_{t_{2}^{-}})=\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
. Also, it holds that
\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{+}}\backslash\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{-}}\subseteq c_{t_{1}^{+}}\subseteq c_{t_{2}^{-}}
. Hence, we have
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})=\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},t_{1}^{+}})\subseteq\sigma(c_{t_{2}^{-}})=\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

Secondly, we prove the sQC case. In
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, for any
i_{1},i_{2}\in[n],h_{1},h_{2}\in[\widetilde{H}]
, suppose agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
(i_{2},h_{2})
.
From the proof above, we know
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
. We only need to prove
\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1}-1
with
t_{1}\in[H]
, then we know
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=m_{i_{1},t_{1}}
. From Assumption
II.1
, we know that
m_{i_{1},t_{1}}\subseteq z_{i_{1},t_{1}}^{a}
. Then, we get
\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{z}_{h_{1}+1})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1},h_{2}=2t_{2}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in[H]
, then from
Lemma

B.2
, we know that
\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1},h_{2}=2t_{2}-1
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in[H]
, then
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}=\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}}
. Hence,
\exists i_{3}\in[n],i_{3}\neq i_{1}
, such that
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}\subseteq\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}+1}\subseteq\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{2}+1}
. Since agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
(i_{2},h_{2})
, we thus know that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
also influences agent
(i_{3},h_{2}+1)
.
Since
\mathcal{L}
is sQC, we know
\sigma(a_{i_{1},t_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{3},t_{2}^{-}})
in
\mathcal{L}
by
Lemma

B.2
. From Assumption
II.2
and
i_{1}\neq i_{3}
, we know
\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})=\sigma(a_{i_{1},t_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(c_{t_{2}^{-}})=\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

This completes the proof.
∎

C-C
Proof of Lemma
IV.3

Proof.

From the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, since
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
requires agents to share more than
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, it is easy to observe that
\forall h\in[\widetilde{H}],i\in[n],\widetilde{c}_{h}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h},\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}\subseteq\breve{\tau}_{i,h}
.

Let
i_{1},i_{2}\in[n],h_{1},h_{2}\in[\widetilde{H}],h_{1}<h_{2}
, and agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
.

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1}-1
with
t_{1}\in[H]
, then
h_{1}
is a communication step. Hence,
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\breve{c}_{h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
and
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=m_{i_{1},t_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{1}+1}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
from Assumption
II.1
. Therefore, we have
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\cup\sigma(\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{c}_{h_{2}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1},h_{2}=2t_{2}-1
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in[H]
, then
\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}=\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
. If agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
(i_{2},h_{2})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, but agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
(i_{2},h_{2})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, then it means
\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}
but
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\notin\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}
. This can only happen when
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{c}_{h_{2}})
, and
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
. Also, from the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, we know that
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\backslash\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{1}}
. Therefore, we have
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\cup\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{c}_{h_{2}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

If agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, then we claim that
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
influences
\widetilde{s}_{h_{1}+1}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, since otherwise,
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
does not influence any
\widetilde{s}_{h},\widetilde{o}_{h}
, and
\widetilde{a}_{h}
with
h>h_{1}
; also, from Assumption
III.5
,
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
is removed from
\widetilde{\tau}_{h},\forall h>h_{1}
, and thus agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
cannot influence agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
. Meanwhile,
from the QC IS of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we know that
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})=\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}})
. Then, from the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, we know that
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
is added in
\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
, i.e.,
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
. Still, due to
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\backslash\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{1}}
, we further have
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\cup\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

•

If
h_{1}=2t_{1},h_{2}=2t_{2}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in[H]
. If agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
(i_{2},h_{2})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, then it means that adding
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
in
\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
via strict expansion leads to such influence. Then, it must hold that
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{c}_{h_{2}})
, and
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
. We can conclude
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\cup\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{c}_{h_{2}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.

If agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
(i_{2},h_{2})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, then we know that
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
influences
\widetilde{s}_{h_{1}+1}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, similarly as shown in the case for
h_{2}=2t_{2}-1
.
Therefore, from Assumption
III.7
, we know that there exists some
i_{3}\neq i_{1}
such that agent
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
influences agent
(i_{3},h_{1}+1)
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then, from the QC IS of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we know that
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1})
. Meanwhile, from Assumption
II.1
(e), we know that
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}\subseteq\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{2}}
. Therefore, we can get
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{2}})
and further
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h_{2}})
due to Assumption
II.2
and
i_{3}\neq i_{1}
. Then, from the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, we know that
\widetilde{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
is added in
\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
. Together with
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\backslash\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{2}}
, we can conclude that
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\cup\sigma(\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{h_{2}}).

This completes the proof.
∎

C-D
Proof of Theorem
IV.4

Proof.

Firstly, we claim that given any strategy
\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}}
and
\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}}=\varphi(\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}})
,
J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}})=J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}})
, where the function
\varphi
is given by
Algorithm

3
. It suffices to prove that
\widetilde{g}_{i,h}(\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h})=\breve{g}_{i,h}(\breve{\tau}_{i,h})
always holds for any
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
. Namely, for any
i\in[n],h\in[\widetilde{H}]
, and
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
,
Algorithm

3
can compute the associated
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}
from the expansion in
Equation

IV.2
, and use it as the input of
\breve{g}_{i,h}
(Line
11
of
Algorithm

3
).
Let
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}^{\prime}
be the information constructed by
Algorithm

3
, which is the
input of
\breve{g}_{i,h}
used in Line 11 of the algorithm. From the construction of the algorithm and the expansion, we have
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}\subseteq\breve{\tau}_{i,h}^{\prime},\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}\subseteq\breve{\tau}_{i,h}
;
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}\backslash\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
and
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}^{\prime}\backslash\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
only consist of some actions. For any action
\breve{a}_{j,t}
with
j\in[n],t<h
, it will be added in
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}^{\prime}\backslash\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
if and only if it lies in
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}\backslash\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
, since we use the same condition in construction in the algorithm as that in the expansion (i.e.,
Equation

IV.2
).

Since
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
has larger strategy spaces, i.e.,
\max_{\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}}\in\widetilde{G}_{1:\widetilde{H}}}J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}})\leq\max_{\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}}\in\breve{G}_{1:\breve{H}}}J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}})
. Let
\breve{g}^{\ast}_{1:\breve{H}}
be the strategy satisfying
J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{g}^{\ast}_{1:\breve{H}})\geq\max_{\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}}\in\breve{G}_{1:\breve{H}}}J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}})-\epsilon
. Then, we have
J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\varphi(\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}}^{\ast},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}))=J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{g}^{\ast}_{1:\breve{H}})\geq\max_{\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}}\in\breve{G}_{1:\breve{H}}}J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}})-\epsilon\geq\max_{\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}}\in\widetilde{G}_{1:\widetilde{H}}}J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\widetilde{g}_{1:\widetilde{H}})-\epsilon
. Thus,
\varphi(\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}}^{\ast},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}})
is an
\epsilon
-team optimal strategy of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
∎

Lemma C.1
.

For any given strategy
\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}}\in\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\breve{H}}
, implementing Algorithm
4
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
is equivalent to implementing
\varphi(\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

As shown in Theorem
IV.4
, Algorithm
3
can compute the associated
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}
and use it as the input of
\breve{g}_{i,h}
(Line 12). Therefore, it suffices to prove that Algorithm
4
can also compute the associated
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}
and use it as the input of
\breve{g}_{i,h}
(Line 6), i.e., Algorithm
5
can output the associated
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}
from
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
and
\breve{g}_{1:h-1}
. We prove this by induction.

Firstly, when
h=1
, it holds for any
i\in[n]
such that
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,1}=\breve{\tau}_{i,1}
. In Algorithm
5
, when
h=1
, it will never enter the for loop, and thus the output is
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,1}=\breve{\tau}_{i,1}
.

Secondly, we assume for any
h<t
, the hypothesis holds. Then, for
h=t
, given any
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,t}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{i,t}
and
\breve{g}_{1:t-1}
, let
\breve{\tau}_{i,t}^{\prime}
be the output of Algorithm
5
. For any
j\in[n],h^{\prime}<t
, if it holds that
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{j,h^{\prime}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{t})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\widetilde{a}_{j,h^{\prime}}\notin\widetilde{\tau}_{i,t}
, then it can compute the associated
\breve{\tau}_{j,h^{\prime}}
from induction hypothesis (Lines 5-6), compute the exact
\breve{a}_{j,h^{\prime}}
based on
\breve{g}_{j,h^{\prime}}
(Line 7), and add it into
\breve{\tau}_{i,h}^{\prime}
(Line 8).
Therefore, we know
\breve{\tau}_{i,t}^{\prime}=\widetilde{\tau}_{i,t}\cup\{\widetilde{a}_{j,h^{\prime}}{\,|\,}\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{j,h^{\prime}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{t})\text{ and }\widetilde{a}_{j,h^{\prime}}\notin\widetilde{\tau}_{i,t}\}=\breve{\tau}_{i,t}
.
By induction, we complete the proof.
∎

Remark C.2
.

The difference between Algorithms
3
and
4
lies as follows. Given any
\breve{g}_{1:\breve{H}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, Algorithm
3
needs to recover the output
\widetilde{a}_{i,h}
of
\widetilde{g}_{i,h}
under all possible input
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{i,h},i\in[n],h\in[\widetilde{H}]
, where the cardinality of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{i,h}
could be exponentially large. Thus, Algorithm
3
may suffer from computational intractability. However, Algorithm
4
only requires recovering the output
\widetilde{g}_{i,h}
under the specific
\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h}
that occurred in the trajectory, which can be implemented in polynomial time.

C-E
Proof of Theorem
IV.5

Proof.

To prove that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
has SI-CIBs, it suffices to prove that for any
h=2,\cdots,\breve{H}
, fix any
h_{1}\in[h-1],i_{1}\in[n]
, and for any
\breve{g}_{1:h-1}\in\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{1:h-1},\breve{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}\in\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
, let
\breve{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime}:=(\breve{g}_{1,h_{1}},\cdots,\breve{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\breve{g}_{n,h_{1}})
and
\breve{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}:=(\breve{g}_{1},\cdots,\breve{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\breve{g}_{h-1})
. If
\breve{c}_{h}
is reachable under both
\breve{g}_{1:h-1}
and
\breve{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}
, then the following holds

\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}^{\prime}_{1:h-1}).

(C.1)

We prove this result case by case as follows:

•

If there exists some
i_{3}\neq i_{1}
such that
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{3},h})
and
\sigma(\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{3},h})
, then from Assumption
II.2
, we know that
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{c}_{h}),\sigma(\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{c}_{h})
. Therefore, there exist deterministic measurable functions
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
such that
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\alpha_{1}(\breve{c}_{h}),\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\alpha_{2}(\breve{c}_{h})
, and further it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\alpha_{1}(\breve{c}_{h}),\alpha_{2}(\breve{c}_{h}),\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}).

The last equality is due to the fact that both the input and output of
\breve{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
are conditioned on.

•

If for any
i_{2}\neq i_{1}
, either
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
or
\sigma(\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
, then agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence any agent
(i_{2},h)
with
i_{2}\neq i_{1}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, since otherwise, due to the sQC IS of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, it must hold that
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
and
\sigma(\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
. Moreover, we claim that such an
h_{1}
has to be even, since otherwise, the agent will be at a communication step, and we must have
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\breve{c}_{h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h}\subseteq\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h}
by Assumption
III.4
and the reformulation in
Equation

IV.1
, and
\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=m_{i_{1},\frac{h_{1}+1}{2}}\in z_{\frac{h_{1}+1}{2}}^{a}=\breve{z}_{h_{1}+1}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h}\subseteq\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h}
by Assumption
II.1
(b), which violates the premise of this case. Let
k_{1}:=h_{1}/2
.
Now, we claim that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence the state
\breve{s}_{h}
nor the information

\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h}
. We prove this case by case as follows:

–

Suppose
h
is even. If agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
\breve{s}_{h_{1}+1}
, then from Assumption
III.7
, there exists some
i_{3}\neq i_{1}
such that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
\breve{o}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}
. However, from Assumption
II.1
(e), we know that
\breve{o}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}=o_{i_{3},\frac{h_{1}}{2}+1}\in\tau_{i_{3},\frac{h_{1}}{2}+1}\subseteq\tau_{i_{3},\frac{h}{2}}=\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h}\subseteq\breve{\tau}_{i_{3},h}
, which means that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{3},h)
, leading to a contradiction. Therefore, we know that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\breve{s}_{h_{1}+1}
, and thus for any
i_{2}\in[n]
, it does not influence
\breve{o}_{i_{2},h_{1}+1}
. Also, from Assumption
III.5
, we know that
\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\notin\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{1}+1}
. Therefore, agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{1}+1}
nor
\breve{a}_{i_{2},h_{1}+1}
. By recursion, we know that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\breve{s}_{h^{\prime}}
nor
\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h^{\prime}}

\forall~i_{2}\in[n],h^{\prime}>h
.

–

Suppose
h
is odd, then
\breve{p}_{h}=\emptyset
by
Equation

IV.1
. If agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
\breve{s}_{h}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, then agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
\widetilde{s}_{h}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, since strict expansion does not change system dynamics. This implies that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
s_{h_{1}+1}
in
\mathcal{L}
, since otherwise, as argued above, it will not influence the later state
\breve{s}_{h}
. Moreover, from Assumption
III.7
, we know that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
also influences
\widetilde{o}_{-i_{1},h}
, i.e., there must exist some
i_{3}\neq i_{1}
such that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
\widetilde{o}_{i_{3},h}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
. From Assumption
II.1
(e), it holds that
\widetilde{o}_{i_{3},h}\in\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h+1}
.
Therefore, agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{3},h+1)
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
. From
Lemma

B.2
, we know
\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},k_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(\tau_{i_{3},k^{-}})
in
\mathcal{L}
, where
k:=(h+1)/2
. Furthermore, from Assumption
II.2
and
i_{3}\neq i_{1}
, it holds that
\sigma(\tau_{i_{1},k_{1}^{-}})\subseteq\sigma(c_{k^{-}})
. Also, from
Equation

IV.1
, it holds that
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\tau_{i_{1},k_{1}^{+}}=\tau_{i_{1},k_{1}^{-}}\cup z_{k_{1}}^{a}
and
z_{k_{1}}^{a}=\widetilde{z}_{h_{1}}\subseteq\widetilde{c}_{h}
. Then, we have
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h})=\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{3},h})
. Based on the strict expansion from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, we can get
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\backslash\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{c}_{h_{1}}\subseteq\breve{\tau}_{i_{3},h}
and
\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\breve{c}_{h}
.
Then, it holds that
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{3},h}),\sigma(\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{3},h})
, which leads to a contradiction to the premise that for any
i_{2}\neq i_{1}
, either
\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
or
\sigma(\breve{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
.
Hence, we know agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence the state
\breve{s}_{h}
. Additionally, for any
i_{2}\neq i_{1}
, since agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence agent
(i_{2},h)
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, and
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h}=\breve{c}_{h}=\breve{\tau}_{i_{2},h}
, then we know that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\breve{\tau}_{i_{1},h}
.

Combining the two cases above, we know that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\breve{s}_{h}
, and does not influence
\breve{\tau}_{i,h},\forall i\in[n]
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
, yielding

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{p}_{h},\breve{c}_{h}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{\tau}_{h}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\{\breve{\tau}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1})

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\{\breve{\tau}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime})=\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}(\breve{s}_{h},\breve{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\breve{c}_{h},\breve{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}).

This completes the proof.
∎

C-F
Proof of
Equation

IV.4

Proof.

Firstly, from the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
and the strategy space
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\overline{H}}
, we know that for any
h\in[H],i\in[n],\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{2h-1}=\breve{\mathcal{C}}_{2h-1},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{i,2h-1}=\breve{\mathcal{A}}_{i,2h-1},\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{i,2h}=\breve{{\mathcal{T}}}_{i,2h},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{i,2h}=\breve{\mathcal{A}}_{i,2h}
. Therefore,
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\overline{H}}=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\breve{H}}
, and finding a team optimal strategy of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
in space
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\overline{H}}
is equivalent to finding a team-optimum of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}
in space
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\breve{H}}
by definition.

Secondly, we will prove that the Dec-POMDP
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
satisfies the information evolution rules. From Assumption
II.1
, it holds that, for any
i\in[n],h\in[\overline{H}]
, if
h=2t-1
with
t\in[H]
, then

\displaystyle\widetilde{z}_{h}=\chi_{t}(\widetilde{p}_{h-1},\widetilde{a}_{h-1},\widetilde{o}_{h}),\qquad p_{i,h}=\xi_{i,t}(\widetilde{p}_{i,h-1},\widetilde{a}_{i,h-1},\widetilde{o}_{i,h});

if
h=2t
with
t\in[H]
, then

\displaystyle\widetilde{z}_{h}=\phi_{t}(p_{h-1},\widetilde{a}_{h-1}),\qquad\widetilde{p}_{i,h}=p_{i,h-1}\backslash\phi_{i,t}(p_{i,h-1},\widetilde{a}_{i,h-1}),

where
\chi_{t},\xi_{i,t}
are fixed transformations and
\phi_{h},\phi_{i,h}
are additional-sharing functions. Recall that we defined
p_{i,2t-1}=p_{i,t^{-}}
for any
i\in[n],t\in[H]
. From the expansion (
Equation

IV.2
), we know
\breve{z}_{h}=\breve{c}_{h}\backslash\breve{c}_{h-1}
. Also, from the refinement, we know
\forall i\in[n],h\in[\overline{H}],\overline{z}_{h}=\breve{z}_{h},\overline{c}_{h}=\breve{c}_{h},\overline{a}_{i,h}=\breve{a}_{i,h}=\widetilde{a}_{i,h},\overline{o}_{i,h}=\breve{o}_{i,h}=\widetilde{o}_{i,h}
, and
\forall t\in[H],\overline{p}_{i,2t-1}=p_{i,t^{-}},\overline{p}_{i,2t}=\widetilde{p}_{i,2t}
.

Then, we can construct
\{\overline{\chi}_{h}\}_{h\in[\overline{H}]},\{\overline{\xi}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n],h\in[\overline{H}]}
accordingly as follows:

•

If
h=2t-1
with
t\in[H]
, we define
\overline{\chi}_{h},\{\overline{\xi}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}
as

\displaystyle\forall i\in[n],\overline{\xi}_{i,h}:=\xi_{i,t},\quad\forall\overline{p}_{h-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{h},

\displaystyle\overline{\chi}_{h}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h}):=\chi_{t}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h})\cup\varrho_{h}^{1}\backslash\varrho_{h}^{2},\text{ where}

\displaystyle\varrho_{h}^{1}:=\{\widetilde{a}_{j,h-1}{\,|\,}\forall j\in[n],\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{j,h-1})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h}),\widetilde{a}_{j,h-1}\text{~influences }\widetilde{s}_{h}\}\backslash\chi_{t}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h})\text{ if $h>1$, otherwise $\emptyset.$}

\displaystyle\varrho_{h}^{2}:=\{\widetilde{a}_{j,h_{0}}{\,|\,}\forall j\in[n],h_{0}<h-1,\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{j,h_{0}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h-1}),\widetilde{a}_{j,h_{0}}\text{ influences }\widetilde{s}_{h_{0}+1}\}\cap\chi_{t}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h}).

Note that there exist some functions
f_{h}^{1},f_{h}^{2}
such that
\varrho_{h}^{1}=f_{h}^{1}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h}),\varrho_{h}^{2}=f_{h}^{2}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h})
. This is because,
\varrho_{h}^{1}\subseteq\{\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\}_{i\in[n]}
; which element
\overline{a}_{j,h-1}
is in
\varrho_{h}^{1}
is based on whether
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{j,h-1})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h})
and whether
\widetilde{a}_{j,h-1}\text{~influences }\widetilde{s}_{h}
, which is a property of the problem
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Similarly,
\varrho_{h}^{2}\subseteq\chi_{t}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h})
, and which element
\overline{a}_{j,h_{0}}
is in
\varrho_{h}^{2}
is based on whether
\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{j,h_{0}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h-1}),\widetilde{a}_{j,h_{0}}\text{ influences }\widetilde{s}_{h_{0}+1}
.
Now we will show that
\overline{\chi}_{h},\{\overline{\xi}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}
satisfy

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{h}=\overline{c}_{h-1}\cup\overline{z}_{h},~~\overline{z}_{h}=\overline{\chi}_{h}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h}),\qquad\text{for each }i\in[n],~~\overline{p}_{i,h}=\overline{\xi}_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h}).

For functions
\{\overline{\xi}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}
, based on the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we know
\overline{p}_{i,h-1}=\widetilde{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1}=\widetilde{a}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h-1}=\widetilde{o}_{i,h-1}
, and
\overline{p}_{i,h}=p_{i,t^{-}}=p_{i,h}
.

For function
\overline{\chi}_{h}
, it is easy to verify that
\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h}=(\overline{c}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h})\backslash(\overline{c}_{h-1}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h-1})
and
\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}=(\overline{c}_{h-1}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h-1})\backslash(\overline{c}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h})
. Together with the fact that
\overline{z}_{h}=\widetilde{z}_{h}\cup(\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h})\backslash(\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h})
, it suffices to show that
\varrho_{h}^{1}=\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h},\varrho_{h}^{2}=\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}
. From the expansion, we know that for any
h^{\prime}\in[\overline{H}],\overline{c}_{h^{\prime}}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h^{\prime}}
only consists of some actions at the even timesteps, since the actions at odd timesteps cannot influence the underlying state. Therefore,
\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h}
and
\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}
only consist of some actions at the even timesteps.

For any
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}},i\in[n],t_{1}<t,
if
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}
influences
\widetilde{s}_{2t_{1}+1}
, then from Assumption
III.7
, there exists
i_{2}\neq i
such that
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}
influences
\widetilde{o}_{i_{2},2t_{1}+1}
and thus influences
\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},2t_{1}+1}
due to Assumption
II.1
(e). From the QC IS of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we know that
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i,2t_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i_{2},2t_{1}+1})
, and thus
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i,2t_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{2t_{1}+1})
due to Assumption
II.2
and
i_{2}\neq i
. Therefore, from
Equation

IV.2
, we know that
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}\in\breve{c}_{2t_{1}+1}
. This means, if any action
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}
influences underlying state
\widetilde{s}_{2t_{1}+1}
, we have
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}\in\breve{c}_{2t_{1}+1}=\overline{c}_{2t_{1}+1}
, since it will be added in
\breve{c}_{2t_{1}+1}
via expansion.

Therefore, if any action
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}=\widetilde{a}_{i,h_{1}},i\in[n],h_{1}<h
is in
(\overline{c}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h})\backslash(\overline{c}_{h-1}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h-1})
, it can only happen if
h_{1}=h-1
. Also, for any
i\in[n]
, if
\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\in\varrho_{h}^{1}
, then
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h-1})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h}),\widetilde{a}_{i,h-1}\text{~influences }\widetilde{s}_{h}
, and furthermore,
\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\notin\chi_{t}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h})
. Then, it means
\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\notin\widetilde{c}_{h}
. Therefore,
\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\in(\overline{c}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h})\backslash(\overline{c}_{h-1}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h-1})
and we proved that
\varrho_{h}^{1}\subseteq\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h}
. Also, for any
i\in[n]
,
\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\in\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h}
only if
\overline{a}_{i,h-1}
is added via expansion and
\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\notin\chi_{t}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h})
. Then, this can only happen if
\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h-1})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h})
and
\widetilde{a}_{i,h-1}\text{~influences }\widetilde{s}_{h}
. Therefore, we proved that
\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h}\subseteq\varrho_{h}^{1}
. Combining the two parts we obtain
\varrho_{h}^{1}=\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h}
.

For any
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}},i\in[n],h_{1}<h
is in
\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}=(\overline{c}_{h-1}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h-1})\backslash(\overline{c}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{c}_{h})
. We will know that
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}\in\overline{c}_{h-1}
, then
h_{1}+1\leq h-1
and
\sigma(\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\overline{c}_{h-1})
. Also, from the proof above, we know
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}
influences the state
\overline{s}_{h_{1}+1}
, then together with
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}\in\widetilde{z}_{h}
, we have
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}\in\{\widetilde{a}_{j,h_{0}}{\,|\,}\forall j\in[n],h_{0}<h-1,\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{j,h_{0}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h-1}),\widetilde{a}_{j,h_{1}}\text{ influences }\widetilde{s}_{h_{1}+1}\}\cap\widetilde{z}_{h}
. Therefore,
\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}\subseteq\varrho_{h}^{2}
. Meanwhile, for any
i\in[n],h_{1}<h
, if
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}\in\varrho_{h}^{2}
, then it holds
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}\in\widetilde{z}_{h}
,
h_{1}<h-1
, and
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}
influences
\overline{s}_{h_{1}+1}
. Then, from above, we know that
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}=\breve{a}_{i,h_{1}}
will be added in
\breve{c}_{h_{1}+1}=\overline{c}_{h_{1}+1}\subseteq\overline{c}_{h-1}
since
h_{1}<h-1
. Then,
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}\notin\overline{z}_{h}=\overline{c}_{h}\backslash\overline{c}_{h-1}
. Therefore,
\overline{a}_{i,h_{1}}\in\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}
, and then
\varrho_{h}^{2}\subseteq\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}
. Combining the two parts we obtain
\varrho_{h}^{2}=\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}
.

•

If
h=2t
with
t\in[H]
, we define
\overline{\chi}_{h},\{\overline{\xi}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}
as

\displaystyle\forall i\in[n],\overline{p}_{i,h-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h}\in\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{i,h},\overline{\xi}_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h})=\overline{p}_{i,h-1}\backslash\phi_{i,t}(\overline{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1})

\displaystyle\forall\overline{p}_{h-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{h},\overline{\chi}_{h}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h})=\phi_{t}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1}).

Now we will show that
\overline{\chi}_{h},\{\overline{\xi}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}
satisfy

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{h}=\overline{c}_{h-1}\cup\overline{z}_{h},~~\overline{z}_{h}=\overline{\chi}_{h}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1},\overline{o}_{h}),\qquad\text{for each }i\in[n],~~\overline{p}_{i,h}=\overline{\xi}_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h}).

For functions
\{\overline{\xi}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}
, based on the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we know
\overline{p}_{i,h-1}=p_{i,t^{-}}=p_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1}=\widetilde{a}_{i,h-1}
, and
\overline{p}_{i,h}=\widetilde{p}_{i,h}
.

For function
\overline{\chi}_{h}
, we know that
\overline{p}_{h-1}=p_{t^{-}}=p_{h-1},\overline{a}_{h-1}=\widetilde{a}_{h-1}
, so it suffices to show that
\widetilde{z}_{h}=\overline{z}_{h}
. As shown above,
\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}
and
\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h}
only consist of some actions at the even timesteps. Moreover, for any action
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}
with
i\in[n],t_{1}<t
that influences
\widetilde{s}_{2t_{1}+1}
, it will be added in
\overline{c}_{2t_{1}+1}
via expansion (if
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}\notin\widetilde{c}_{2t_{1}+1}
); if
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}
does not influence
\widetilde{s}_{2t_{1}+1}
, then it will never be added via expansion. Therefore,
\overline{z}_{h}\backslash\widetilde{z}_{h}=\emptyset
. Also, if some action
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}\in\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}
, then we know
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}\in\widetilde{z}_{h}=\phi_{t}(\overline{p}_{h-1},\widetilde{a}_{h-1})
, and further we know
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}\in\overline{p}_{h-1}=p_{i,t^{-}}
. However, if
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}
influences
\widetilde{s}_{2t_{1}+1}
, then it will be added in
\overline{c}_{2t_{1}+1}
and cannot lie in
\overline{p}_{h-1}
; if
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}
does not influence
\widetilde{s}_{2t_{1}+1}
, then from Assumption
III.5
,
\widetilde{a}_{i,2t_{1}}\notin p_{i,t^{-}}
. Therefore, we know
\widetilde{z}_{h}\backslash\overline{z}_{h}=\emptyset
, and get
\widetilde{z}_{h}=\overline{z}_{h}
.

Thirdly, we prove that such a Dec-POMDP
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
has SI-CIBs with respect to the strategy space
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\overline{H}}
. This is equivalent to that
for any
h\in[2:\overline{H}],\overline{s}_{h}\in\overline{{\mathcal{S}}},\overline{p}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h},\overline{c}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{h},i_{1}\in[n],h_{1}<h,\overline{g}_{1:h-1},\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{i_{1}:h_{1}}
, let
\overline{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime}:=(\overline{g}_{1,h_{1}},\cdots,\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\overline{g}_{n,h_{1}})
and
\overline{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}:=(\overline{g}_{1},\cdots,\overline{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\overline{g}_{h-1})
. If
\overline{c}_{h}
is reachable from both
\overline{g}_{1:h-1}
and
\overline{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}
, it holds that
