\displaystyle\text{if $h$ is even, then }\overline{\chi}_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h})=\chi_{i,\frac{h+1}{2}}(\overline{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h})\cup\varrho_{i,h}^{1}\backslash\varrho_{i,h}^{2},\text{ where}

\displaystyle\varrho_{i,h}^{1}:=\{\widetilde{a}_{i,h-1}{\,|\,}\forall\sigma(\widetilde{\tau}_{i,h-1})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h}),\widetilde{a}_{i,h-1}\text{~influences }\widetilde{s}_{h}\}\backslash\chi_{i,\frac{h+1}{2}}(\overline{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h})\text{ if $h>1$, otherwise $\emptyset.$}

\displaystyle\varrho_{i,h}^{2}:=\{\widetilde{a}_{i,h_{0}}{\,|\,}\forall h_{0}<h-1,\sigma(\widetilde{a}_{i,h_{0}})\subseteq\sigma(\widetilde{c}_{h-1}),\widetilde{a}_{i,h_{0}}\text{ influences }\widetilde{s}_{h_{0}+1}\}\cap\chi_{i,\frac{h+1}{2}}(\overline{p}_{i,h-1},\overline{a}_{i,h-1},\overline{o}_{i,h}).

•

If
\mathcal{L}
has a baseline sharing protocol as one of
Examples 2, 4, 7, 8
, or one of
Examples 1, 5, 6
with additional condition
1
) in §
A
, then we replace Equation (B.1) of
[
21
, Algorithm 5]
by:

\displaystyle\text{if $h=2t-1$, we do not make any change,}

(C.26)

\displaystyle\text{if $h=2t$, }\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H}}),z}(\overline{z}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h},{\gamma}_{ct(h),h})\leftarrow\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{p}_{h},\overline{a}_{ct(h),h},\overline{o}_{h+1}}\mathds{1}[\overline{z}_{h+1}=\overline{\chi}_{h+1}(\overline{p}_{i,h},\overline{a}_{ct(h),h},\overline{o}_{h+1})]\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H}})}(\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})

\displaystyle\gamma_{ct(h),h}(\overline{a}_{ct(h),h}{\,|\,}\overline{p}_{h})\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H}})}(\overline{o}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h},\overline{p}_{h},\overline{a}_{ct(h),h}),

and replace Equation (B.2) of
[
21
, Algorithm 5]
by:

\displaystyle\text{if $h=2t-1$, we do not make any change,}

(C.27)

\displaystyle\text{if $h=2t$, }\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H}})}_{i,h}(\widehat{c}_{h},{\gamma}_{i,h})\leftarrow\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{p}_{h},\overline{a}_{i,h}}\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H}})}(\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{i,h}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H}})}(\widehat{c}_{h},\overline{p}_{h},\overline{a}_{i,h})\gamma_{i,h}(\overline{a}_{i,h}{\,|\,}\overline{p}_{i,h}).

•

If
\mathcal{L}
has a baseline sharing protocol as
Example 3
or one of
Examples 1
and
5
with additional condition
3
) in §
A
, then we do not make any change.

Then, the
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H},j})\}_{j=1}^{K}
learned in Line
10
of
Algorithm

2
satisfies that: for any
j\in[K]
,
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H},j})
satisfies the
Factorized structures
condition in §
G-B
if
the baseline sharing protocol of
\mathcal{L}
is one of
Examples 1, 5, 6
with additional condition
2
);
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H},j})
satisfies the
Turn-based structures
condition in §
G-B
, if the baseline sharing protocol of
\mathcal{L}
is one of
Examples 2, 4, 7, 8
or
Examples 1, 5, 6
with additional condition
1
);

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(g^{1:\overline{H},j})
satisfies the
Nested private information
condition in §
G-B
, if the baseline sharing protocol of
\mathcal{L}
is
Example 3
or
Examples 1, 5
with additional condition
3
). From Lemma
G.2
, we can conclude that Assumption
IV.7
holds, by noticing that in these examples,
\max_{h\in[\overline{H}]}|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h}|
depends polynomially on the parameters of the original LTC problem
\mathcal{L}
. Hence,
Algorithm

2
has the following sample and time complexities

•

Examples 1, 3, 5, 6:

\texttt{poly}\left(\max_{h\in[\overline{H}]}(|\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{h}||\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h}|)^{C\gamma^{-4}\log(\frac{|\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}|}{\epsilon})},|\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}|,\overline{H},\frac{1}{\epsilon},\log(\frac{1}{\delta})\right)
;

•

Examples 2, 4, 7, 8:

\texttt{poly}\left(\max_{h\in[\overline{H}]}(|\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{h}||\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h}|)^{C\gamma^{-4}\log(\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}|}{\epsilon})+2d},|\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}|,\overline{H},\frac{1}{\epsilon},\log(\frac{1}{\delta})\right)
,

to achieve
\max_{\overline{g}_{1:\overline{H}}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\overline{H}}}J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}(\overline{g}_{1:\overline{H}})-J_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}(\overline{g}_{1:\overline{H}}^{\widehat{j},\ast})\leq\epsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
,
which completes the proof of
Part III
.

Combining
Parts I, II, III
, we complete the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Deferred Details of §
V

In the following part, we will use

\overline{\phantom{x}}

to denote the elements and random variables in the Dec-POMDP
\mathcal{D}
. We first introduce the notion of
perfect recall

[
18
]
:

Definition D.1

(Perfect recall)
.

We say that agent
i
has perfect recall if
\forall h=2,\cdots,\overline{H}
,
it holds that
\overline{\tau}_{i,h-1}\cup\{\overline{a}_{i,h-1}\}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{i,h}.

If for any
i\in[n]
, agent
i
has perfect recall, we call that the Dec-POMDP has a perfect recall property.

D-A
Proof of Theorem
V.1

Proof.

sQC
\Rightarrow
SI-CIB:

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a Dec-POMDP with an sQC information structure that satisfies Assumptions
II.1
(e),
II.2
,
III.5
, and
III.7
. To prove that
\mathcal{D}
has SI-CIBs, it is sufficient to prove that for any
h=2,\cdots,\overline{H}
, fix any
h_{1}\in[h-1],i_{1}\in[n]
, and for any
\overline{g}_{1:h-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:h-1},\overline{g}^{\prime}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
, let
\overline{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime}:=(\overline{g}_{1,h_{1}},\cdots,\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\overline{g}_{n,h_{1}})
and

\overline{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}:=(\overline{g}_{1},\cdots,\overline{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\overline{g}_{h-1})
, for any
\overline{c}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{h}
reachable under both
\overline{g}_{1:h_{1}},\overline{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}
,
the following holds:

\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h-1}),

(D.1)

where, for notational simplicity, we omit both the superscript
\mathcal{D}
and the subscript
h
for the beliefs throughout this proof, as they are clear from the context.
We prove this case by case as follows:

•

If there exists some
i_{2}\neq i_{1}
such that

\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\cup\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
, then from Assumption
II.2
, we know that
\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\cup\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\overline{c}_{h})
. Therefore, there exist deterministic measurable functions
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
such that
\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\beta_{1}(\overline{c}_{h}),\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}=\beta_{2}(\overline{c}_{h})
, and it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}_{1:h-1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\beta_{1}(\overline{c}_{h}),\beta_{2}(\overline{c}_{h}),\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}},\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}},\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}).

The last equality is because the input and output of
\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
(and
\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}
) are
\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
and
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
, respectively, and they are both conditioned on.

•

If
for all
i_{2}\neq i_{1}
,
either
\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
or
\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h})
, then agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence agent
(i_{2},h)
for any
i_{2}\neq i_{1}
, since
\mathcal{D}
is sQC.

Firstly, we claim that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\overline{s}_{h_{1}+1}
: if it influences, from Assumption
III.7
, there exists some
i_{3}\neq i_{1}
such that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences
\overline{o}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}
; however, from Assumption
II.1
(e), we know
\overline{o}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}\in\overline{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{i_{3},h}
; therefore, agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{3},h)
, contradicting the premise above.
Similarly, we have that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\overline{s}_{h_{2}}
for any
h_{2}>h_{1}
.

Secondly, we claim that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\overline{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{2}}
, for any
i_{3}\in[n]
and
h_{2}>h_{1}
. Since agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\overline{s}_{h_{1}+1}
, then by Assumption
III.5
, for any
h_{2}>h_{1}
,
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\notin\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}
and agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\overline{o}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}
for any
i_{3}\in[n]
, which implies that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence any element in
\overline{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}
for any
i_{3}\in[n]
, either directly or indirectly. Since
\overline{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}
is the input of agent
i_{3}
’s strategy at timestep
h_{1}+1
to decide
\overline{a}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}
, agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
thus does not influence
\overline{a}_{i_{3},h_{1}+1}
for any
i_{3}\in[n]
, either, which, together with the fact that it does not influence
\overline{s}_{h_{1}+2}
and thus not
\overline{o}_{i_{3},h_{1}+2}
for any
i_{3}\in[n]
, further implies that it does not influence any element in
\overline{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{1}+2}
for any
i_{3}\in[n]
. By recursion, agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\overline{\tau}_{i_{3},h_{2}}
for any
i_{3}\in[n]
and
h_{2}>h_{1}
.

Therefore, agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
does not influence
\overline{c}_{h}=\cap_{i_{3}=1}^{n}\overline{\tau}_{i_{3},h}
nor
\overline{p}_{h}=\overline{\tau}_{h}\backslash\overline{c}_{h}
, and it does not influence
\overline{s}_{h}
, either, which means that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h-1}).

SI-CIB
\Rightarrow
sQC:

Since
\mathcal{D}
has perfect recall and has SI-CIBs, we know that
\forall i\in[n],h\in[\overline{H}],\forall\overline{g}_{1:h-1},\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:h-1},\overline{c}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{h},\overline{s}_{h}\in\overline{{\mathcal{S}}},\overline{p}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h}
, if
\overline{c}_{h}
is reachable under both
\overline{g}_{1:h-1},\overline{g}_{1:h-1}^{\prime}
, then the following holds:

\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}_{1:h-1})=\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h},\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h-1}).

Our goal is to prove that
if agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
in the intrinsic model of the Dec-POMDP
\mathcal{D}
,
then under any strategy
\overline{g}_{1:\overline{H}}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:\overline{H}}
,
\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\cup\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
holds.
Note that throughout the proof, when it comes to
\sigma
-algebra inclusion, we meant it up to the null sets generated by
\overline{g}_{1:\overline{H}}
.
We prove this by contradiction. If this is not true, then there exist
i_{1},i_{2}\in[n],h_{1},h_{2}\in[\overline{H}]
, such that agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
influences agent
(i_{2},h_{2})
, but either
\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
or
\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
.
First, we know that
i_{2}\neq i_{1}
, since otherwise it always holds that
\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{2}}
and
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{2}}
, due to the perfect recall assumption.
Then, we
discuss the following different cases.

•

If
\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
, then it implies that
\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\overline{c}_{h_{2}})
, since
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}
.
This further implies that there must exist a strategy
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}
and some realizations
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{h_{2}},\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\neq\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0,\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
and
\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
. This further leads to that
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\notin\overline{c}_{h_{2}}
. Hence, due to perfect recall, there must exist some realizations
\overline{p}_{h_{2}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h_{2}},\overline{s}_{h_{2}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
such that
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\overline{p}_{h_{2}}
, and
\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
. Then, we define another strategy
\overline{g}^{\prime}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
as:

\forall\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{i_{1},h_{1}},~~~\overline{g}^{\prime}_{i_{1},h_{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})=\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},

(D.2)

and we let
\overline{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime}:=(\overline{g}_{1,h_{1}},\cdots,\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\overline{g}_{n,h_{1}})
and

\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime}:=(\overline{g}_{1},\cdots,\overline{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\overline{g}_{h_{2}-1})
. Now we claim that
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}
has non-zero probability under
\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1}
, i.e.,
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}
is reachable under
\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1}
. Since
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}
is reachable under
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}
and

\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
, we know that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
. Since
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime}
only differs from
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}
in the strategy of agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
, and
\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}
always chooses
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}
, then it holds that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime})\geq\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
, and thus
\mathbb{P}(\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime})>0
.
Meanwhile, due to (
D.2
), we have
\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1})=0\neq\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}),

which leads to a contradiction to the fact that
\mathcal{D}
has SI-CIBs.

•

If
\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
, then it must be that
\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\nsubseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
. This further implies that there must exist a strategy
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}
and some realizations
\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\neq\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{i_{1},h_{1}},\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{i_{2},h_{2}}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0,\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}{\,|\,}\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
, and
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}^{\prime}_{i_{1},h_{1}}{\,|\,}\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
. Then, due to perfect recall, there exist some realizations
\overline{s}_{h_{2}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{S}}},\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{h_{2}}
such that
\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}},\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}
and
\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
. Let
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h_{2}}
be the realizations such that
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}
and
\overline{p}_{h_{2}}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}
, then it holds that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}},\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})=\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
. Thus, we know that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
, and that
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}
is reachable under
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
.
Meanwhile, since
\sigma(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}})\subseteq\sigma(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})
, we know that
there exists a realization
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
such that

\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}{\,|\,}\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}},\overline{g}^{\circ}_{1:h_{2}-1})=1
holds for any strategy
\overline{g}^{\circ}_{1:h_{2}-1}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1:h_{2}-1}
. By applying
\overline{g}^{\circ}_{1:h_{2}-1}=\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}
, we obtain
\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}{\,|\,}\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})=1
.
Consider another different action realization
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}\neq\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
, and define a new strategy
\overline{g}^{\prime}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
as

\overline{g}^{\prime}_{i_{1},h_{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}})=\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},~~~\overline{g}^{\prime}_{i_{1},h_{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime})=\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}},

(D.3)

and keep
g^{\prime}_{i_{1},h_{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime\prime})
the same as
g_{i_{1},h_{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime\prime})
for any other realizations
\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime\prime}
. We denote
\overline{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime}:=(\overline{g}_{1,h_{1}},\cdots,\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\overline{g}_{n,h_{1}})
and

\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime}:=(\overline{g}_{1},\cdots,\overline{g}_{h_{1}}^{\prime},\cdots,\overline{g}_{h_{2}-1})
. Since
\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}
has non-zero probability under
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}
, and
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}{\,|\,}\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
, it holds that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
. Together with the fact that
\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}{\,|\,}\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})=1
, we can obtain that

\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}},\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
. Since
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime}
only differs from
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}
in the strategy of agent
(i_{1},h_{1})
, and
\overline{g}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}
always chooses
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
when inputting
\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime}
, then it holds that

\mathbb{P}(\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime})\geq\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime})\geq\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}},\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime})\geq\mathbb{P}(\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}^{\prime},\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}},\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1})>0
, which means
\overline{c}_{h_{2}}
is reachable under
\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}^{\prime}
. Meanwhile, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1})=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}},\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1})}{\mathbb{P}(\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1})}=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1})}{\mathbb{P}(\overline{c}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1})}=0\neq\mathbb{P}(\overline{s}_{h_{2}},\overline{p}_{h_{2}}{\,|\,}\overline{c}_{h_{2}},\overline{g}_{1:h_{2}-1}),

(D.4)

where the third equality is because 1)
\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}
, and
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}\in\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}
since
\mathbb{P}(\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}}{\,|\,}\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}})=1
,
\overline{\tau}_{i_{2},h_{2}}\subseteq\overline{\tau}_{h_{2}}
, and perfect recall; 2)
\overline{a}_{i_{1},h_{1}},\overline{\tau}_{i_{1},h_{1}}
can never be realized simultaneously under
\overline{g}^{\prime}_{1:h_{2}-1}
due to (
D.3
). Therefore, (
D.4
) leads to a contradiction to the fact that
\mathcal{D}
has SI-CIBs.

This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Decentralized POMDPs (with Information Sharing)

A Dec-POMDP with
n
agents and potential information sharing can be characterized by a tuple

\mathcal{D}=\langle H,{\mathcal{S}},\{\mathcal{A}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n],h\in[H]},\{\mathcal{O}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n],h\in[H]},\{\mathbb{T}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]},\{\mathbb{O}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]},\mu_{1},\{\mathcal{R}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}\rangle,

where
H
denotes the length of each episode,
{\mathcal{S}}
denotes the state space, and
\mathcal{A}_{i,h}
denotes the
control action
space of agent
i
at timestep
h
. We denote by
s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}
the state and by
a_{i,h}
the control action of agent
i
at timestep
h
. We use
a_{h}:=(a_{1,h},\cdots,a_{n,h})\in\mathcal{A}_{h}:=\mathcal{A}_{1,h}\times\mathcal{A}_{2,h}\times\cdots\mathcal{A}_{n,h}
to denote the joint control action for all the
n
agents at timestep
h
, with
\mathcal{A}_{h}
denoting
the joint control action space at timestep
h
. We denote
\mathbb{T}=\{\mathbb{T}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
the collection of transition functions, where

\mathbb{T}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{h},a_{h})\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
gives the transition probability to the next state
s_{h+1}
when taking the joint control action
a_{h}
at state
s_{h}
. We use
\mu_{1}\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
to denote the distribution of the initial state
s_{1}
. We denote by
\mathcal{O}_{i,h}
the observation space and by
o_{i,h}\in\mathcal{O}_{i,h}
the observation of agent
i
at timestep
h
. We use

o_{h}:=(o_{1,h},o_{2,h},\cdots,o_{n,h})\in\mathcal{O}_{h}:=\mathcal{O}_{1,h}\times\mathcal{O}_{2,h}\times\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n,h}
to denote the joint observation of all the
n
agents at timestep
h
, with
\mathcal{O}_{h}
denoting
the joint observation space at timestep
h
. We use
\{\mathbb{O}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
to denote the collection of emission matrices, where
o_{h}\sim\mathbb{O}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{h})\in\Delta(\mathcal{O}_{h})
at timestep
h
under state
s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}
. For notational convenience, we adopt the matrix convention, where
\mathbb{O}_{h}
is a matrix with each row
\mathbb{O}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{h})
for all
s_{h}\in{\mathcal{S}}
. Also, we denote by
\mathbb{O}_{i,h}
the marginalized emission for agent
i
at timestep
h
. Finally,
\{\mathcal{R}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
is a collection of reward functions among all the agents, where
\mathcal{R}_{h}:{\mathcal{S}}\times\mathcal{A}_{h}\rightarrow[0,1]
.

At timestep
h
,
each agent
i
in the Dec-POMDP has access to some
information
\tau_{i,h}
, a subset of historical joint observations and actions, namely,
\tau_{i,h}\subseteq\{o_{1},a_{1},o_{2},\cdots,a_{h-1},o_{h}\}
, and the collection of all possible such available information is denoted by
{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h}
. We use
\tau_{h}
to denote the
joint
available information at timestep
h
.
Meanwhile, agents may
share
part of the history with each other. The
common information

c_{h}=\cup_{t=1}^{h}z_{t}
at timestep
h
is thus a subset of the joint history
\tau_{h}
, where
z_{h}
is the information shared at timestep
h
. We use
\mathcal{C}_{h}
to denote the collection of all possible
c_{h}
at timestep
h
, and use
{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h}
to denote the collection of all possible
\tau_{i,h}
of agent
i
at timestep
h
. Besides the common information
c_{h}
, each agent also has her
private information

p_{i,h}=\tau_{i,h}\backslash c_{h}
, where the collection of
p_{i,h}
is denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{i,h}
. We also denote by
p_{h}
the
joint
private information, and by
\mathcal{P}_{h}
the collection of all possible
p_{h}
at timestep
h
. We refer to the above the
state-space model
of the Dec-POMDP (with information sharing), which follows the models in
[
28
,
27
]
.

Each agent
i
at timestep
h
chooses the control action
a_{i,h}
based on some strategy
g_{i,h}:{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{i,h}
. We denote by
g_{h}:=(g_{1,h},g_{2,h},\cdots,g_{n,h})
the joint control strategy of all the agents, and by
g_{1:h}:=(g_{1},g_{2},\cdots,g_{h}),\forall h\in[H]
the sequence of joint strategies from timestep
1
to
h
. We use
\mathcal{G}_{i,h}
to denote the strategy space of
g_{i,h}
, and use
\mathcal{G}_{h},\mathcal{G}_{1:h}
to denote joint strategy spaces, correspondingly.
Next, we introduce some background on the intrinsic model and information structure of Dec-POMDPs.

F-A
Intrinsic Model

In an intrinsic model
[
37
]
, we regard the agent
i
at different timesteps as
different agents
, and each agent only acts
once
throughout.
Any Dec-POMDP
\mathcal{D}
with
n
agents can be formulated within the intrinsic-model framework, and can be characterized by a tuple

\langle(\Omega,\mathscr{F}),N,\{(\mathbb{U}_{l},\mathscr{U}_{l})\}_{l=1}^{N},\{(\mathbb{I}_{l},\mathscr{I}_{l})\}_{l=1}^{N}\rangle

[
23
]
, where
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
is a measurable space of the environment,

N=n\times H
is the number of agents in the intrinsic model. By a slight abuse of notation, we write
[N]:=[n]\times[H]
, and write
l:=(i,h)\in[N]
for notational convenience. This way, any agent
l\in[N]
corresponds to an agent
i\in[n]
at timestep
h\in[H]
in the state-space model.
We denote by
\mathbb{U}_{l}
the measurable action space of agent
l
and by
\mathscr{U}_{l}
the
\sigma
-algebra over
\mathbb{U}_{l}
. For
A\subseteq[N]
, let
\mathbb{H}_{A}:=\Omega\times\prodop\displaylimits_{l\in A}\mathbb{U}_{l}
and
\mathbb{H}:=\mathbb{H}_{[N]}
. For any
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{C}
over
\mathbb{H}_{A}
, let
\langle\mathscr{C}\rangle
denote the cylindrical extension of
\mathscr{C}
on
\mathbb{H}
.
Let
\mathscr{H}_{A}:=\langle\mathscr{F}

\otimes

(\otimes_{l\in A}\mathscr{U}_{l})\rangle
and
\mathscr{H}=\mathscr{H}_{[N]}
.
We denote by
\mathbb{I}_{l}
the space of
information available
to agent
l
, and by
\mathscr{I}_{l}
the
\sigma
-algebra over
\mathbb{H}
. For
l\in[N]
, we denote by
I_{l}
the information of agent
l
, and
U_{l}
the action of agent
l
.
The spaces and random variables of agent
l=(i,h)
in the intrinsic model are related to those in the state-space model as follows:

\forall l=(i,h)\in[N],\mathbb{U}_{l}=\mathcal{A}_{i,h},\mathbb{I}_{l}={\mathcal{T}}_{i,h},U_{l}=a_{i,h},I_{l}=\tau_{i,h}
.

F-B
Information Structures of Dec-POMDPs

An important class of IS is the
quasi-classical
one, which is defined as follows
[
37
,
23
,
39
]
.

Definition F.1

(Quasi-classical Dec-POMDPs)
.

We call a Dec-POMDP problem
QC
if each agent in the intrinsic model knows the information available to the agents who influence her, directly or indirectly, i.e.,
\forall l_{1},l_{2}\in[N],l_{1}=(i_{1},h_{1}),l_{2}=(i_{2},h_{2}),i_{1},i_{2}\in[n],h_{1},h_{2}\in[H]
, if agent
l_{1}
influences agent
l_{2}
, Then,
\mathscr{I}_{l_{1}}\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{l_{2}}
.

Furthermore,
strictly
quasi-classical IS
[
37
,
24
]
, as a subclass of QC IS, is defined as follows.

Definition F.2

(Strictly quasi-classical Dec-POMDPs)
.

We call a Dec-POMDP problem
sQC
if each agent in the intrinsic model knows the information
and
actions available to the agents who influence her, directly or indirectly. That is,
\forall l_{1},l_{2}\in[N],l_{1}=(i_{1},h_{1}),l_{2}=(i_{2},h_{2}),i_{1},i_{2}\in[n],h_{1},h_{2}\in[H]
, if agent
l_{1}
influences agent
l_{2}
, Then,
\mathscr{I}_{l_{1}}\cup\langle\mathscr{U}_{l_{1}}\rangle\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{l_{2}}
.

F-C
Intrinsic Model of LTC Problems

Given any LTC
\mathcal{L}
of the state-space-model form defined in §
II-A
, we define the intrinsic model of
\mathcal{L}
as a tuple
\langle(\Omega,\mathscr{F}),N,\{(\mathbb{U}_{l},\mathscr{U}_{l})\}_{l=1}^{N},\{(\mathbb{M}_{l},\mathscr{M}_{l})\}_{l=1}^{N},\{(\mathbb{I}_{l^{-}},\mathscr{I}_{l^{-}})\}_{l=1}^{N},\{(\mathbb{I}_{l^{+}},\mathscr{I}_{l^{+}})\}_{l=1}^{N}\rangle
, where
(\Omega,\mathscr{F})
is the measure space representing all the uncertainty in the system;
N=n\times H
is the number of agents in the intrinsic model. By a slight abuse of notation, we write
[N]:=[n]\times[H]
, and write
l:=(i,h)\in[N]
for convenience. This way, any agent
l\in[N]
corresponds to an agent
i\in[n]
at timestep
h\in[H]
in the state-space model, and we thus define
l^{-}:=(i,h^{-})
and
l^{+}:=(i,h^{+})
accordingly.
We denote by
\mathbb{U}_{l}
and
\mathbb{M}_{l}
the measurable control and communication action spaces of agent
l
, and by
\mathscr{U}_{l}
and
\mathscr{M}_{l}
the
\sigma
-algebra over
\mathbb{U}_{l}
and
\mathbb{M}_{l}
, respectively.
For any
A\subseteq[N]
, let
\mathbb{H}_{A}:=\Omega\times\prodop\displaylimits_{l\in A}(\mathbb{U}_{l}\times\mathbb{M}_{l})
and
\mathbb{H}:=\mathbb{H}_{[N]}
. For any
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{C}
over
\mathbb{H}_{A}
, let
\langle\mathscr{C}\rangle
denote the cylindrical extension of
\mathscr{C}
on
\mathbb{H}
.
Let

\mathscr{H}_{A}:=\langle\mathscr{F}

\otimes

(\otimes_{l\in A}\mathscr{U}_{l})

\otimes

(\otimes_{l\in A}\mathscr{M}_{l})\rangle
,
\mathscr{H}=\mathscr{H}_{[N]}
.
We denote by
\mathbb{I}_{l^{-}}
and
\mathbb{I}_{l^{+}}
the spaces of
information available
to agent
l

before
and
after
additional sharing, respectively,
and by
\mathscr{I}_{l^{-}}\subseteq\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{I}_{l^{+}}\subseteq\mathscr{H}
the associated
\sigma
-algebra.
The spaces and random variables of agent
l=(i,h)
in the intrinsic model are related to those in the state-space model as follows:

\forall l=(i,h)\in[N],\mathbb{U}_{l}=\mathcal{A}_{i,h},\mathbb{M}_{l}=\mathcal{M}_{i,h},\mathbb{I}_{l^{-}}={\mathcal{T}}_{i,h^{-}},\mathbb{I}_{l^{+}}={\mathcal{T}}_{i,h^{+}},U_{l}=a_{i,h},M_{l}=m_{i,h},I_{l^{-}}=\tau_{i,h^{-}},I_{l^{+}}=\tau_{i,h^{+}}
. For notational convenience, for any random variable
B
in LTC and the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
generated by
B
, we overload
\sigma(B)
to denote the cylindrical extension of
\mathscr{B}
on
\mathbb{H}
, i.e.,
\sigma(B)=\langle\mathscr{B}\rangle
.

Appendix G
Other Supplementary Results

G-A
Optimality of Deterministic Strategies

We now show a supplementary result that for the formulated LTC problem, it does not lose optimality to consider
deterministic
strategies as introduced in §
II
.
For any LTC problem
\mathcal{L}
, consider generic, stochastic communication and control strategy spaces:
\forall i\in[n],~h\in[H],~\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{m,S}:=\{g_{i,h}^{m,S}:{}_{i,h}^{m}\times{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h^{-}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{i,h}\},~\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{a,S}:=\{g_{i,h}^{a,S}:{}_{i,h}^{a}\times{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h^{+}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{i,h}\}
, where
\{{}_{i,h}^{m}\}_{i\in[n],h\in[H]},\{{}_{i,h}^{a}\}_{i\in[n],h\in[H]}
are the sets of random seeds which could be correlated to each other across agents and timesteps.
Note that these strategy classes include those of the strategies randomized over the action sets, i.e.,
\forall i\in[n],~h\in[H],~\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{m,S}:=\{g_{i,h}^{m,S}:{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h^{-}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{M}_{i,h})\},~\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{a,S}:=\{g_{i,h}^{a,S}:{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h^{+}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i,h})\}
.
Also, we denote by
\mathcal{G}_{h}^{a,S},\mathcal{G}_{h}^{m,S}
the
joint
stochastic control and communication spaces at timestep
h
, respectively.
Similarly, we define the objective under the stochastic strategies as

\forall g_{1:H}^{a,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a,S},g_{1:H}^{m,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m,S},\qquad\qquad J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a,S}_{1:H},g^{m,S}_{1:H}):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{L}}\left[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{H}(r_{h}-\kappa_{h}){\,\bigg|\,}g^{a,S}_{1:H},g^{m,S}_{1:H}\right].

Lemma G.1
.

It does not lose optimality to consider deterministic control and communication strategies in LTC. Namely, for any LTC problem
\mathcal{L}
,

\max_{g_{1:H}^{a,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a,S},g_{1:H}^{m,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m,S}}J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a,S}_{1:H},g^{m,S}_{1:H})=\max_{g_{1:H}^{a}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m}}J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a}_{1:H},g^{m}_{1:H}).

(G.1)

Proof.

For any
i\in[n],h\in[H]
, since space
\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{m,S}
covers space
\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{m}
and
space
\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{a,S}
covers space
\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{a}
, we have that

\displaystyle\max_{g_{1:H}^{a,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a,S},g_{1:H}^{m,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m,S}}J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a,S}_{1:H},g^{m,S}_{1:H})\geq\max_{g_{1:H}^{a}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m}}J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a}_{1:H},g^{m}_{1:H}).

(G.2)

In the other direction, from the tower property, for any
g_{1:H}^{a,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a,S},g_{1:H}^{m,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m,S}

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a,S}_{1:H},g^{m,S}_{1:H})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{L}}\left[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{H}(r_{h}-\kappa_{h}){\,\bigg|\,}g^{a,S}_{1:H},g^{m,S}_{1:H}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{L}}\big[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{H}(r_{h}-\kappa_{h}){\,\bigg|\,}\{g^{a,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{a}]\}_{i\in[n],h\in[H]},\{g^{m,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{m}]\}_{i\in[n],h\in[H]}\big]\right],

where
\omega_{i,h}^{a}\in{}_{i,h}^{a}
,
\omega_{i,h}^{m}\in{}_{i,h}^{m}
are random seeds, and
g_{i,h}^{a,S}[\omega_{i,h}^{a}]\in\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{a},g^{a,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{m}]\in\mathcal{G}_{i,h}^{m}
are deterministic strategies defined as

\displaystyle\forall\tau_{i,h^{-}}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h^{-}},g^{m,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{m}](\tau_{i,h^{-}}):=g^{m,S}_{i,h}(\omega_{i,h}^{m},\tau_{i,h^{-}}),

\displaystyle\forall\tau_{i,h^{+}}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i,h^{+}},g^{a,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{a}](\tau_{i,h^{+}}):=g^{a,S}_{i,h}(\omega_{i,h}^{a},\tau_{i,h^{+}}).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a,S}_{1:H},g^{m,S}_{1:H})=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{L}}\big[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{H}(r_{h}-\kappa_{h}){\,\bigg|\,}\{g^{a,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{a}]\},\{g^{m,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{m}]\}\big]\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\max_{\{\omega_{i,h}^{a}\}_{i\in[n],h\in[H]},\{\omega_{i,h}^{m}\}_{i\in[n],h\in[H]}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{L}}\left[\sumop\displaylimits_{h=1}^{H}(r_{h}-\kappa_{h}){\,\bigg|\,}\{g^{a,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{a}]\},\{g^{m,S}_{i,h}[\omega_{i,h}^{m}]\}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\max_{g_{1:H}^{a}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m}}J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a}_{1:H},g^{m}_{1:H})

holds for any
g_{1:H}^{a,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a,S},g_{1:H}^{m,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m,S}
. Hence, we further get

\displaystyle\max_{g_{1:H}^{a,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a,S},g_{1:H}^{m,S}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m,S}}J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a,S}_{1:H},g^{m,S}_{1:H})\leq\max_{g_{1:H}^{a}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{a},g_{1:H}^{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{1:H}^{m}}J_{\mathcal{L}}(g^{a}_{1:H},g^{m}_{1:H}).

(G.3)

Combining
Equation

G.2
and
Equation

G.3
, we prove
Equation

G.1
.
∎

G-B
Conditions Leading to Assumption
IV.7

As a minimal requirement for computational tractability (for both Dec-POMDPs and LTCs), Assumption
IV.7
is needed for the one-step tractability of the team-decision problem involved in the value iteration in Algorithm
6
. We now adapt several such structural conditions from
[
21
]
to the LTC setting, which lead to this assumption and have been studied in the literature. Note that since we need to do planning in the approximate model
\mathcal{M}
, which is oftentimes constructed based on the original problem
\mathcal{L}
and the approximate beliefs
\{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})\}_{h\in[\overline{H}]}
, we necessarily need conditions on models
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
, and the beliefs
\{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})\}_{h\in[\overline{H}]}
that
\mathcal{M}
is consistent with, to ensure Assumption
IV.7
holds, for which we refer to as the
Part (1)
and
Part (2)
of the conditions below, respectively.

•

Turn-based structures.

Part (1):
At each timestep
h\in[\overline{H}]
, there is only one agent, denoted as
ct(h)\in[n]
, that can affect the state transition. More concretely, the transition dynamics take the forms of
\mathbb{T}_{h}:{\mathcal{S}}\times\mathcal{A}_{ct(h),h}\rightarrow\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
. Additionally, we assume the reward function admits an additive structure such that
\mathcal{R}_{h}(s_{h},a_{h})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{R}_{i,h}(s_{h},a_{i,h})
for some functions
\{\mathcal{R}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Meanwhile, since only agent
ct(h)
takes the action, we assume the increment of the common information satisfies
z_{h+1}^{b}=\chi_{h+1}(p_{h^{+}},a_{ct(h),h},o_{h+1})
.
Part (2):
No additional requirement. Such a structure has been commonly studied in (fully observable) stochastic games and multi-agent RL
[
8
,
3
]
.

•

Nested private information.

Part (1):
No additional requirement.
Part (2):
At each timestep
h=2t,t\in[H]
, all the agents form a
hierarchy
according to the private information they possess, in the sense that
\forall~i,j\in[n],j<i,\overline{p}_{j,h}=Y_{h}^{i,j}(\overline{p}_{i,h})
for some function
Y_{h}^{i,j}
. More formally, the approximate belief satisfies that
\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{p}_{j,h}=Y_{h}^{i,j}(\overline{p}_{i,h}){\,|\,}\overline{p}_{i,h},\widehat{c}_{h})=1
. Such a structure has been investigated in
[
31
]
with heuristic search, and in
[
21
]
with finite-time complexity analysis when there is no additional sharing to decide/learn.

•

Factorized structures.

Part (1):
At each timestep
h\in[\overline{H}]
, the state
s_{h}
can be partitioned into
n
local states, i.e.,
s_{h}=(s_{1,h},s_{2,h},\cdots,s_{n,h}).
Meanwhile, the transition kernel takes the product form of
\mathbb{T}_{h}(s_{h+1}{\,|\,}s_{h},a_{h})=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{T}_{i,h}(s_{i,h+1}{\,|\,}s_{i,h},a_{i,h})
, the emission also takes the product form of
\mathbb{O}_{h}(o_{h}{\,|\,}s_{h})=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{O}_{i,h}(o_{i,h}{\,|\,}s_{i,h})
, and the communication cost and reward functions can be decoupled into
n
terms such that
\mathcal{K}_{h}(z_{h}^{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{K}_{i,h}(z_{i,h}^{a}),\mathcal{R}_{h}(s_{h},a_{h})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{R}_{i,h}(s_{i,h},a_{i,h})
.

Part (2):
At each timestep
h\in[\overline{H}]
, the approximate common information is also factorized so that
\widehat{c}_{h}=(\widehat{c}_{1,h},\widehat{c}_{2,h},\cdots,\widehat{c}_{n,h})
and its evolution satisfies that
\widehat{c}_{i,h}=\widehat{\phi}_{i,h}(\widehat{c}_{i,h-1},\overline{z}_{i,h-1})
for some function
\widehat{\phi}_{i,h}
. Correspondingly, the approximate beliefs need to satisfy that
\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{i,h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{i,h},\overline{p}_{i,h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{i,h})
for some functions
\{\mathbb{P}_{i,h}^{\mathcal{M},c}\}_{i\in[n],h\in[\overline{H}]}
.
Such a structure, under general information sharing protocols, can lead to non-classical IS. In this case, it may be viewed as an example of
non-classical ISs where the agents have no incentive for signaling
[
39
, §3.8.3]
.

Lemma G.2
.

Given any LTC problem
\mathcal{L}
, let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
be the Dec-POMDP after reformulation, strict expansion, and refinement. For any
\mathcal{M}
to be the approximate model of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
and
\{\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}\}_{h\in[\overline{H}]}
to be the approximate belief, if they satisfy any of the
3
conditions above, Then,
Equation

E.1
in Algorithm
6
can be solved with time complexity
\max_{h\in[\overline{H}]}\text{poly}~(|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h}|,|\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h}|,|\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}|)
.

Proof.

For any
h\in[\overline{H}]
, if
h=2t-1,t\in[H]
, from Assumption
III.4
and the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
, since we need to find the optimal strategy of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
in the spaces
\overline{g}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{h}=\{g:\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{h}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h}\}
for all
h\in[\overline{H}]
, where we recall that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h}=\mathcal{M}_{t}
is joint communication action space. Then,
\Gamma_{h}=\mathcal{M}_{t}
has cardinality
|\Gamma_{h}|=|\mathcal{M}_{t}|
, and
\gamma_{1:n,h}^{\ast}
can be computed as
\gamma_{1:n,h}^{\ast}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\gamma_{1:n,h}\in\Gamma_{h}}Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},\gamma_{1:n,h})

by enumerating all possible
\gamma_{1:n,h}\in\Gamma_{h}
, with a complexity of
|\mathcal{M}_{t}|
.

If
h=2t,t\in[H]
, we prove the result case by case:

•

Nested private information:

We first define the
u_{i,h}\in\mathcal{U}_{i,h}:=\{(\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i}\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{j,h})\times(\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i-1}\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{j,h})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{i,h}\}
and slightly abuse the notation for
Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}
as follows

\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},u_{1,h},\cdots,u_{n,h}):=

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h},\overline{a}_{h},\overline{s}_{h+1},\overline{o}_{h+1}}\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}[\overline{a}_{i,h}=u_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{1:i,h},\overline{a}_{1:i-1,h})]\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{h},\overline{a}_{h})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{h+1}(\overline{o}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{h+1})[\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{a}_{h},\overline{p}_{h})+V_{h+1}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h+1})].

Since the space of
\mathcal{U}_{i,h}
covers the space of
\Gamma_{i,h}
, Then, for the
u_{1:n,h}^{\ast}
to be an optimal one that maximizes the
Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},u_{1,h}^{\ast},\cdots,u_{n,h}^{\ast})=\max_{\{u_{i,h}\in\mathcal{U}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}}Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},u_{1,h},\cdots,u_{n,h})\geq\max_{\{\gamma_{i,h}\in\Gamma_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}}Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},\gamma_{1,h},\cdots,\gamma_{n,h}).

Meanwhile, due to the nested private information condition, for any
\overline{p}_{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h}
, there must exist
\gamma_{1:n,h}^{\prime}
such that
\gamma_{1:n,h}^{\prime}
output the same actions as
u_{1:n,h}^{\ast}
under
\overline{p}_{h}
. Therefore, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\max_{\{u_{i,h}\in\mathcal{U}_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}}Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},u_{1,h},\cdots,u_{n,h})=\max_{\{\gamma_{i,h}\in\Gamma_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}}Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},\gamma_{1,h},\cdots,\gamma_{n,h}).

Therefore, we can solve
Equation

E.1
and compute
\gamma_{1:n,h}^{\ast}
from computing
u_{1:n,h}^{\ast}
, which can be solved with complexity poly
(|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h}|,|\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h}|,|\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}|)
.

•

Turn-based structures:
For any
\gamma_{ct(h),h}\in\Gamma_{ct(h),h},\gamma_{-ct(h),h},\gamma_{-ct(h),h}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{-ct(h),h}
, where
ct(h)
is the controller at timestep
h
, it holds that for any
\widehat{c}_{h}
:

\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},\gamma_{ct(h),h},\gamma_{-ct(h),h})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h},\overline{s}_{h+1},\overline{o}_{h+1}}\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{h},\gamma_{ct(h),h}(\overline{p}_{ct(h),h}),\gamma_{-ct(h),h}(\overline{p}_{-ct(h),h}))

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{h+1}(\overline{o}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{h+1})[\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h},\gamma_{h}(\overline{p}_{h}))+V_{h+1}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h+1})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h},\overline{s}_{h+1},\overline{o}_{h+1}}\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{h},\gamma_{ct(h),h}(\overline{p}_{ct(h),h})\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{h+1}(\overline{o}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{h+1})[\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h},\gamma_{ct(h),h}(\overline{p}_{ct(h),h}))+V_{h+1}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h+1})],

\displaystyle\qquad+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\neq ct(h)}\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}}\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i,h}(\overline{s}_{h},\gamma_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,h})):=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}U_{i,h}(\widehat{c}_{h},\gamma_{i,h}),

where the last step is due to the fact that
\widehat{c}_{h+1}=\widehat{\phi}_{h+1}(\widehat{c}_{h},\overline{z}_{h+1})
. Note that we can write
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{a}_{h},\overline{p}_{h})
as
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{a}_{h})
since
h
is even.
Then,
Equation

E.1
can be solved with respect to each individual
\gamma_{i,h}
with total complexity poly
(|\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}|,|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h}|,|\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{h}|)
.

•

Factorized structures:

Note that for any
h\in[\overline{H}]
, we can write the reward function of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
as
\overline{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{a}_{h},\overline{p}_{h})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\overline{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{s}_{i,h},\overline{a}_{i,h},\overline{p}_{i,h}),\forall\overline{s}_{h},\overline{a}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}
. Then,
for any
h\in[\overline{H}]
,
\widehat{c}_{h}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{h},\gamma_{h}\in\Gamma_{h}
, we use backward induction to prove that, there exist
n
functions
\{F_{i,h}\}_{i\in[n]}
such that

Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},\gamma_{h})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}F_{i,h}(\widehat{c}_{i,h},\gamma_{i,h}).

It holds for
h=\overline{H}
since

Q_{\overline{H}}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{\overline{H}},\gamma_{\overline{H}})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{s}_{i,\overline{H}},\overline{p}_{i,\overline{H}}}\mathbb{P}_{i,\overline{H}}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{i,\overline{H}},\overline{p}_{i,\overline{H}}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{i,\overline{H}})\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i,\overline{H}}(\overline{s}_{i,\overline{H}},\gamma_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,\overline{H}}),\overline{p}_{i,\overline{H}})
.
For any
h\leq\overline{H}-1
, it holds that

\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\ast,\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{c}_{h},\gamma_{h})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h},\overline{s}_{h+1},\overline{o}_{h+1}}\mathbb{P}_{h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{h},\overline{p}_{h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{h})\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{h},\gamma_{h}(\overline{p}_{h}))\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{h+1}(\overline{o}_{h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{h+1})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\bigg[\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i,h}(\overline{s}_{i,h},\gamma_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,h}),\overline{p}_{i,h})+F_{i,h+1}(\widehat{c}_{i,h+1},\widehat{g}_{i,h+1}^{\ast}(\widehat{c}_{i,h+1}))\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\sumop\displaylimits_{\overline{s}_{i,h},\overline{p}_{i,h},\overline{s}_{i,h+1},\overline{o}_{i,h+1}}\mathbb{P}_{i,h}^{\mathcal{M},c}(\overline{s}_{i,h},\overline{p}_{i,h}{\,|\,}\widehat{c}_{i,h})\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{h}(\overline{s}_{i,h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{i,h},\gamma_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,h}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{i,h+1}(\overline{o}_{i,h+1}{\,|\,}\overline{s}_{i,h+1})[\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i,h}(\overline{s}_{i,h},\gamma_{i,h}(\overline{p}_{i,h}),\overline{p}_{i,h})+F_{i,h+1}(\widehat{c}_{i,h+1},\widehat{g}_{i,h+1}^{\ast}(\widehat{c}_{i,h+1}))]

\displaystyle\quad=:\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}F_{i,h}(\widehat{c}_{i,h},\gamma_{i,h}).

Then, by induction, we know that it holds for any
h\in[\overline{H}]
. We can define
\widehat{g}_{i,h}^{\ast}(\widehat{c}_{h})\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\gamma_{i,h}\in\Gamma_{i,h}}F_{i,h+1}(\widehat{c}_{i,h+1},\gamma_{i,h})
, and thus solve
Equation

E.1
with complexity
\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}
poly
(|\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{i}|,|\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{i,h}|,|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{i,h}|)
.

This completes the proof.
∎

Note that, strictly speaking, the time-complexity given in
Lemma

G.2
does not satisfy Assumption
IV.7
yet, since
|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h}|
may not be necessarily small and polynomial in the LTC parameters
|{\mathcal{S}}|,|\mathcal{O}_{h}|,|\mathcal{A}_{h}|,|\mathcal{M}_{h}|,H
. For the examples in §
A
, more specifically, one can show that
|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{h}|
is indeed polynomial in these parameters (when viewing the delay
d
of sharing, if it exists, as a constant), which led to the final quasi-polynomial complexity guarantees in
Theorem

IV.8
and
Theorem

IV.9
(see their proofs for the formal arguments).

Appendix H
Examples in the Venn Diagram
Figure

2(b)

Here, we show some examples of the areas ①-⑤ in the Venn diagram in
Figure

2(b)
. Note that, unless otherwise noted, the diagram here concerns the common scenarios where
the transition kernel
\{\mathbb{T}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
and observation emission
\{\mathbb{O}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
are
non-degenerate
, i.e., for any agent
i\in[n]
and timestep
h\in[H]
, the change of state
s_{h}
can influence the observation
o_{i,h}
, and the action
a_{i,h}
can influence the state
s_{h+1}
.

•

①: Multi-agent MDP
[
6
]
with historical states.
The Dec-POMDPs satisfying that for any
h\in[H],i\in[n],\mathcal{O}_{i,h}={\mathcal{S}},\mathbb{O}_{i,h}(s{\,|\,}s)=1,c_{h}=s_{1:h},p_{h}=\emptyset
lie in the area ①.

•

②: Uncontrolled state process without any historical information.
The Dec-POMDPs satisfying that for any
h\in[H],i\in[n],s_{h},a_{h},a_{h}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{h},a_{h})=\mathbb{T}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{h},a_{h}^{\prime}),c_{h}=\emptyset,p_{i,h}=\{o_{i,h}\}
lie in the area ②.

•

③:

Dec-POMDPs with sQC information structure, perfect recall, and Assumptions
III.5
and
III.7
.
One-step delayed information sharing (
Example 1
in §
A
) lies in this area.

•

④: State controlled by one controller with no sharing and only observability of the controller.
We consider a Dec-POMDP
\mathcal{D}
. The state dynamics are controlled by only one agent (for convenience, agent 1), and only agent 1 has observability, i.e.,
\mathbb{T}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{h},a_{1,h},a_{-1,h})=\mathbb{T}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{h},a_{1,h},a_{-1,h}^{\prime})
for all
s_{h},a_{1,h},a_{-1,h},a_{-1,h}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{O}_{-1,h}=\emptyset
. There is no information sharing, i.e.,
c_{h}=\emptyset,p_{1,h}=\{o_{1:h},a_{1:h-1}\},p_{j,h}=\{a_{j,1:h-1}\},\forall j\neq 1
. Then,
\forall j\neq 1,h_{1}<h_{2}\in[H]
, agent
(1,h_{1})
does not influence
(j,h_{2})
, since
\tau_{j,h_{2}}=\{a_{j,1:h_{2}-1}\}
is not influenced by agent
(1,h_{1})
. Therefore,
\mathcal{D}
is sQC and has perfect recall, but
\mathcal{D}
is not SI, since the underlying state
s_{h}
is influenced by
g_{1,1:h-1}
. This is essentially because
\mathcal{D}
does not satisfy Assumption
III.7
. Thus,
\mathcal{D}
lies in the area ④.

•

⑤: One-step delayed observation sharing and two-step delayed action sharing.
The Dec-POMDPs satisfying that for any
h\in[H],i\in[n],c_{h}=\{o_{1:h-1},a_{1:h-2}\},p_{i,h}=\{a_{i,h-1},o_{i,h}\}
lie in the area ⑤.
